# Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (Chapter 7) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of the Heights**

Al-Aʿrāf, "the Heights," is the seventh chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 206 verses, and it stands as one of the great mid-length Makkan sūrahs whose structure resembles a vast architectural plan: a broad statement of the Law, then a series of rooms — narrative suites in which the sacred history of the prophets is told again and again, each time widening the circle of warning. The name comes from the word *al-aʿrāf* in verses 46 and 48, the "heights" from which certain men will look across a partition between the Garden and the Fire, identifying the people of both sides "by their marks." Classical commentators differ on what these heights are: some say a ridge or terrace between Paradise and Hell, others say a level of Paradise from which its inhabitants survey all that is below. Whatever the precise identification, the image gives the sūrah its name and one of its deepest psychological tones: the standing, unresolved middle — the place of those whose outcome is not yet the reward they hoped for, who can see the blessed and the damned, and who must watch. It is a name that keeps the sūrah, and everyone who reads it, out of comfort.

**A Late Makkan Composition with a Subtler Edge**

In the order of revelation al-Aʿrāf is counted among the later Makkan sūrahs, and many scholars place it just before al-Anʿām, with some authorities reporting that verses 163–167 were revealed in Madinah. Its immediate audience is the same as al-Anʿām's: the idolatrous Quraysh who refused to accept Muḥammad ﷺ as a prophet. But where al-Anʿām argues directly with the arguments of its opponents, al-Aʿrāf takes a subtler, less polemical course. It does not meet the Makkans point for point; instead it turns them toward the past and says, in effect: look at what your story has always looked like, from the first of the prophets to the last of the cities. The sūrah's central thesis, stated and restated in many forms, is that the consequences of rejecting the signs of God are not hypothetical; they are historical, and they are repeatable. The chapters of sacred history — Adam, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāli, Lūt, Shuʿayb, Mūsā and Pharaoh — are arranged not as antiquarian material but as a courtroom record, each case closed the same way.

**The Shape of the Sūrah**

The opening verses (1–10) state the case of the Book: it is sent down for the Prophet's breast, for warning, and for the remembrance of the believers; it is followed by the fate of destroyed towns, by the questioning of messengers and their peoples, and by the weighing of deeds. Then the first great narrative begins (11–28): the creation of Adam, the refusal of Iblīs, the temptation in the Garden, the descent, and the clothing that follows. From the clothing the sūrah moves to adornment and temperance (31–33), to the appointed term of every nation (34–39), and then to the famous passage of the Hereafter in which the people of the Garden call out to the people of the Fire, and the men on the Heights call to those they know by their marks (44–50). A brief discourse on the signs of God in creation (52–58) introduces the long procession of the destroyed peoples (59–93): Noah, the people of the well and the palm-grove (Hūd), the she-camel of Ṣāli, the transgression of the people of Lūt, and the commerce and religion of Shuʿayb. The Mūsā narrative (103–156) is the sūrah's longest and most detailed suite: the staff and the white hand, the confrontation with Pharaoh, the magicians, the plagues, the parting of the sea, the drowning, the mountain, the golden calf, and the covenant of the elders. A verse identifies the Prophet as the "unlettered" Muḥammad ﷺ written in the Torah and the Gospel (157). The sūrah then returns to Israel's later history — the breach of the covenant, the punishment of the Sabbath-breakers, the city in the land (161–67) — and closes, after a passage on the character of the Prophet and his mission (158–188), with the great verses of the Covenant of the Looms (172), the story of the two brothers and the field (176–77), the parable of the dog in the Cave (178–81), and the final, serene vision of the angels in the Presence of God, who are not too proud to worship Him, glorifying Him and prostrating to Him (206). The arc of the sūrah — from the letters that open the Book to the angels that close it — is deliberate: it begins with the mystery of Divine speech and ends with the order of Divine worship, and between the two, every human story that could carry the warning is told at length.

**The Method of Its Warning**

What distinguishes al-Aʿrāf from its neighbors is the patience of its method. It does not once say "you Makkans will be destroyed" in so many words in the narrative sections; it simply tells the case files and lets the listener recognize himself. This is a warning designed to bypass the defender's defenses: when a man is told directly that he is wrong, he prepares his answer; when he is told a story of a people he knows, he watches it, and in the watching, he confesses. The sūrah trusts its evidence and its order. It also pairs the narratives with the present address constantly — "O Children of Adam" — so that every story is also a mirror. The result is a sūrah that reads less like a sermon than like a history written by God for the use of His creatures, in which the past is not the past but a live demonstration, and in which the last page, the page of the angels and of the final reckoning, is the only page that is already finished.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:1

> **Alif. Lām. Mīm. Ṣād.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Letters, and Only These, Before This Book**

The seventh sūrah of the Qur'an does not begin with a word, a name, a blessing, or a story. It begins with four letters of the Arabic alphabet, recited by their own names in that order: *Alif, Lām, Mīm, Ṣād* — a combination that belongs to this sūrah alone. Of the twenty-nine sūrahs that open with the so-called "separated letters" (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*), no other carries precisely this quartet. Alif, Lām, Mīm together open the second, third, and twenty-ninth chapters; Mīm alone opens others; and ṣād opens chapter thirty-eight. But the four-letter sequence *ʾalif lām mīm ṣād* stands in the muṣḥaf exactly once, and that once is here, at the gate of al-Aʿrāf. The repetition of the formula across the Book establishes that the letters are not a scribal accident but a standing feature of Divine speech in the Qur'an; the singularity of this particular combination then marks this sūrah with its own signature, as though the Book announced, before a single sentence was spoken, that what follows is a specific and unrepeatable message.

**A Signature of Mystery**

The commentators' first instinct was the same as it was for every set of the separated letters: to acknowledge that the letters are true, real, and weighty, and to acknowledge that their precise meaning is one of the points in which God has concealed knowledge from us. It is transmitted among the early authorities that the meaning of the letters is a *munkarat* — a mystery that the Prophet ﷺ did not explain and that therefore was not to be forced. Some of the later reporters, as the sources gather them, held that the opening letters are a name of God, and that this particular sequence may stand for the "Greatest Name" (*al-Ism al-Aʿẓam*), the name by which God is known to Himself before He is known to creation. Others held that the letters are a name for the Qur'an itself, the Book signing its own opening with the raw material of its own language. Neither opinion is demonstrable; both are preserved in the tradition as reverent conjecture. The soundest course, and the one the older commentators consistently favored, is to hold what is certain: that God opened His Book with these letters, that no meaning has been authentically fixed to them, and that their silence at the threshold is itself a teaching.

**The Challenge Under the Surface**

What can be said with confidence is the function the letters serve in the pattern of the Book. In most of the sūrahs that open with the separated letters, the very next verse mentions the Book or the revelation: *Alif Lām Mīm, this is the Book of which there is no doubt*; *Alif Lām Mīm, a Book We have sent down unto you*; and here, *Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād — [this is] a Book sent down unto thee*. The letters, in other words, are the herald of the Book, the sound the Book makes before it speaks. And there is a second, deeper function that the later commentators drew out at length. The letters are the very letters of the Arab's own tongue. The Qur'an was revealed in the Arabic of the Quraysh, and the Quraysh were the masters of Arabic, the people whose poetry was the measure of eloquence. By opening sūrah after sūrah with the alphabet itself, the revelation performs a quiet demonstration: here are your own letters, your own sounds, your own material, in the hand of your own tongue's Speaker — and from this material, a Book of this order. The explicit challenge, *bring a sūrah like it*, comes elsewhere; here the challenge is implied by the opening itself, the way a master archer lays a bow at the feet of his rivals without saying a word.

**A Pause Before the Storm**

There is also a spiritual function, one the practical commentators of the devotional strain emphasized. A sūrah that opens with isolated letters forces the reciter into a moment of pure voice before meaning: the lips form sounds that do not yet signify, and in that gap the heart is suspended between the letter and the word, between the sound and the sense. For the worshipper this is a small discipline with a large effect. The sūrah that follows these four letters is among the sternest in the Qur'an — it will recount destroyed cities, a fallen devil, a drowned pharaoh, a people cursed in their fields and their sabbaths. To approach it, God has placed a pause: four letters, no meaning, only Presence. The listener is given, in the first second of the sūrah, the posture the entire sūrah demands — the posture of a witness who has not yet heard the verdict. That is the office of these letters at the gate of al-Aʿrāf: to make the reader still, to mark the Book as a Book from above, and to open the page.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:2

> **[This is] a Book sent down unto thee — so let there be no constriction in thy breast because of it — that thou mayest warn thereby, and a Reminder for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Book Named at Its Own Gate**

The four letters of the opening are not left hanging in the air. The very next word names what they herald: *This is a Book* (*hādhā kitābun*). Grammarians note the figure in the phrase: the noun *kitāb* is in the state of explanation (*ṣila al-bayān*) to the demonstrative *hādhā*, as though the Book is so near that it is pointed at, so present that it is shown rather than described. And it is immediately qualified with the passive participle *anẓala* — "sent down" — which does two things at once: it declares the Book's origin (it is not produced, compiled, or composed by any hand in this world) and its direction (it descends, it comes from above, it is addressed downward to the human level). The combination — "this is a Book sent down unto thee" — is the Qur'an's own self-introduction in miniature, and it is the first complete sentence of the sūrah. Everything that follows in al-Aʿrāf — the destroyed towns, the prophets, the Hereafter — is the unfolding of a single claim that is made in this verse: that a Book has come from God, and that it has come to one man first, and through him to the rest of the world.

**The Constriction in the Breast**

The address now turns to the Prophet ﷺ, and it turns to the most sensitive place in him: *so let there be no constriction in thy breast because of it*. The word is *ḥaraj* or, in the common reading, the sense of *ḍayq* — a narrowing, a tightening, a closing-in, as when a chest constricts under fear or grief so that the breath grows short. The Qur'an elsewhere places the *ṣadr*, the breast or chest, precisely as the organ of this kind of distress: it is where the word that is too heavy to bear "weighs down" (2:255, where the recitation of the signs is a *ṣadr ḍīq* — "a narrow breast"), and it is where the heart sits. The Qur'an's psychology is bodily before it is abstract: the distress of the messenger is not a thought but a physical tightening, the kind of compression a person feels in the pit of the chest when he sees his own people turn away from the very thing that could save them.

This is not a hypothetical. The history of the first years of the mission is a history of constriction. The Prophet ﷺ had come down from the cave of Ḥirā with a breast that, by his own account to Khadija, was afraid for itself; he had stood before the Quraysh and seen the faces of men he loved harden against him; he had been stoned, insulted, and blocked in the streets of Makkah. The constriction this verse speaks of is not doubt about the Book — that question never troubled him — it is the sorrow and fear of the messenger who carries a warning his people will refuse. And the command "let there be no constriction" is not a command to stop feeling; it is a command to locate the feeling correctly. The Qur'an elsewhere treats exactly this kind of messenger's distress with the same medicine: *do not grieve over them* (16:127), *do not be in distress* (28:21, 56), and the famous prayer taught for the very condition — when a believer's heart tightens, he is taught to say, "O God, Lord of the Throne, Lord of the Might, Lord of the Greatness" — a dhikr whose name in the sources is the *dhikr al-ḍiqq*, the remembrance for constriction. The cure for a constricted breast is not less feeling but the transfer of the feeling to its proper object: the One who owns the constriction and can open it.

**Warning as Purpose, Remembrance as Result**

The verse then states the Book's double office in one sentence. First: *that thou mayest warn thereby*. The root is *n-z-r*, and the word *nadhīr* carries the sense of one who gives notice of a coming harm, the one who stands at the gate and says, a caravan of trouble is coming, and the door is this. Warning is forward-looking: it addresses a future that can still be avoided. The Book was sent down, on this account, so that the Prophet would perform the function of the warners — *nadhīr* is one of his standing titles in the Qur'an, as it is of Noah, Hud, and all the prophets told the same story in this sūrah. Second: *and a Reminder for the believers*. The word is *dhikrā*, from the root *d-h-r*, remembrance. If warning looks forward to the punishment that is avoided, remembrance looks present and inward: it is the keeping of God in the heart, the repeated turning back to the source. The same Book therefore performs two different functions on two different hearers. For the one who has not entered the path, it is a warning: it shows the fate of the destroyed towns and the weighing of deeds. For the one who has entered, it is a remembrance: it keeps the heart warm, the direction steady, and the gratitude fresh. The sentence is a complete map of the Qur'an's relationship to its two audiences, and it is a map that the sūrah will then spend 205 verses walking.

**The Logic of the Command**

There is a logic here worth stating plainly, for it is the logic that holds the whole prophetic enterprise together. The Book was sent down "unto thee" — to one man — and the reason given for not letting his breast constrict is the task itself: *that thou mayest warn*. The messenger must be free of the constriction of fear of his people's answer, because a messenger whose chest is closed by the fear of rejection will deliver a diminished warning, or delay it, or soften it into something his people can swallow. God's command is thus a command about the conditions of effective communication: the message must be carried whole. And the same law, by the pattern of the verse, extends beyond the Prophet to every person who has received the message and carries it. The believer who speaks the truth he has been given and feels his breast constrict at the cost he will pay — the cost of the family's disapproval, the office's disfavor, the crowd's laughter — is addressed by the same verse in the same way: the constriction is real, the feeling is not a sin, and the cure is to place the feeling before the One who sees the breast and opens it. The verse, in other words, is not only a Makkan command to one man; it is a standing statute for every messenger of the Book in every age, including the private age in which the message is carried by an individual rather than a state.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:3

> **Follow that which has been sent down unto you from your Lord, and follow not any protectors apart from Him. Little do you reflect!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Widens**

The previous verse spoke to the Prophet in the singular. This verse speaks in the plural, and the shift is deliberate: the command now reaches the community, and beyond the community, all people. *Follow that which has been sent down unto you from your Lord* — the "you" is the collective "you" of every person who has received the Book and stood in the light of it. The object of the command is the revealed Book, the very Book named in the preceding verse, and the verb is *ittabiʿū*, "follow," from the root *ṭ-w-y*, which pictures walking behind someone on a path: to follow is to place oneself on the same track as the one you follow, to let his footfall set your pace. To "follow" a revealed Book is therefore not an abstract approval of its truth but a practical alignment: one makes the Book the route of one's life, the direction of one's choices, the standard by which one measures what to take and what to leave. The Qur'an uses the same verb for the following of the prophets and the following of the law, and the pattern is constant: faith in the Book without following it is like knowing the map and walking a different road.

**Protectors Besides God**

The negative half of the verse is the one that carries the weight: *and follow not any protectors apart from Him*. The word is *awliyāʾ*, the plural of *walī*, from the root *w-l-y*, which means closeness — a wali is literally a close one, and the word has grown in Arabic to cover the whole family of relations: the guardian, the patron, the ally, the protector, the friend who has authority over a person or a cause. To "take" (*ittakhadhū*) protectors apart from God is to place oneself in a relation of closeness, dependence, and loyalty toward something or someone other than God, such that one's security, direction, and love are attached to that other thing. The Qur'an returns to this phrase again and again, and each use clarifies the range of what the "protectors" can be. The disbelievers "took their idols as protectors" (2:257); men "took their satans as protectors apart from God" (18:50, 43:36); and in this very sūrah, a few verses later, the same sentence is repeated with the subject made explicit: *they took satans as their protectors apart from God and deem them rightly guided* (7:30). But the classical commentators broadened the phrase further than the literal, and rightly so. In the early Muslim community the "protectors" apart from God were often not idols at all but human beings — the associates, friends, and kindred who incited the Companions to their old religion, whose approval was the real currency of safety, and whose disapproval was the real punishment. The *walī* in this verse is whatever stands between a person and God in the distribution of his loyalty, whether it is a carved stone, a demon of the unseen, or a living human being whose pleasure is sought as a god is sought.

The Qur'an gives the logic of the error in one of its sharpest parables, in Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt:

> *"The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew."* (29:41)

The image is precise. The spider believes in the strength of its web; the web is the strongest thing in the world to the spider, and the frailest in the world to everything else. The "protectors" apart from God are exactly this: they feel like the whole of security to the person attached to them, and they are the weakest possible thing at the moment of need. When the hour comes in which a protector is tested — the illness, the loss, the death, the hour in which the money cannot be commanded and the status cannot be summoned — the web is cut, and the one who took the spider for a house has no house. The verse's command is therefore not a matter of doctrine but of engineering: attach your security to that which is actually strong.

**The Rebuke of the Unreflecting**

The verse closes with a rebuke that is short and very old in the Qur'an: *Little do you reflect!* The word is *taʿqilūn*, from the root *ʿ-q-l*, the same root as *ʿaql*, the intellect, and the word *taʿqīl* — to reason, to judge, to grasp, to hold a thing in the mind and weigh it. The rebuke is not against the existence of the intellect but against its non-use. The logic of the whole verse is a piece of reasoning: if the Book is from God, and if God is the only protector that is real, then to follow the Book and to take no other protector is the only rationally consistent position; to follow the Book in words while attaching one's life to other protectors is to hold two incompatible directions at once. And the person who does this is not ignorant; he is one who has chosen not to reflect. The Qur'an repeats this exact rebuke in the same short form in several places — "Little do you reflect!" (27:62, 40:58), "Do you not reflect?" (69:42, and dozens of close variants) — and the repetition is itself a teaching: the central failure of the human heart is not a lack of intelligence but a refusal to apply it to the things that matter.

**The Two Loyalties in Modern Form**

The verse's structure — one positive command and one negative prohibition, joined by the logic of exclusive loyalty — is the structure of every real allegiance in human life. A citizen who follows the constitution and also follows a criminal gang is not a citizen in any sense, even if the gang is beloved and the constitution is distant. A person who says he serves God and serves money in the same hour is in the same position, and the Qur'an's phrase for it is the one in this verse: he has taken *awliyāʾ* apart from God. The modern form of the disease is not less real than the Makkah form; it is only better dressed. The "protectors" of a modern life — the career that must not be risked, the reputation that must not be damaged, the crowd that must not be opposed, the money that must not be questioned — are taken in exactly the way the Quraysh took their idols: not as objects of worship in a temple, but as objects of dependence in the heart, the things one actually turns to when the crisis comes. And the cure is the one the verse names: *taʿqīl*, reflection, the simple and brutal test that every real protector fails — *what will this thing do for me in the hour in which I am alone?* The idol, the status, the money, the friend all fail that test; only the One the verse names passes it. To reflect, in the sense this verse requires, is to run that test honestly, and to let the result arrange one's loyalties.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:4

> **How many a town have We destroyed! Our Might came upon them by night, or while they took their ease at midday.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetoric of the Many**

The verse opens with a rhetorical plural in the form of a question: *How many a town have We destroyed!* The Arabic word is *kaẓīr*, and the figure is the one the grammarians call the *mabnāʾ li-ḍamīr al-kathīrah* — a form that does not expect an answer, because the answer is not a number but a volume: many, so many that counting has stopped being possible. The construction is deliberate. The Qur'an does not say "I destroyed your town" or "I destroyed one town"; it says *how many*, and the listener's mind is immediately placed in front of a procession of cities, a city after a city, each one with its own name and its own end. This is the sūrah announcing its own method. The long narrative suite that follows — the people of Noah, the people of Hud, the people of Salih, the people of Lot, the people of Shuayb, and then the people of Pharaoh — is the gallery that this single verse opens. The verse is the door of the exhibit: step in, and the walls are lined with the cases of the destroyed towns.

The "town" (*qaryah*) is the unit of the Qur'an's moral history. In the Qur'an, sin is not primarily an individual act in the modern sense; it is a communal condition. A town is destroyed, not a man; a city is struck, not a household. The reason is logical: the corruption that merits destruction is never the corruption of one man but the corruption of a whole order of life — the judges who have sold judgment, the markets that have cheated by measure, the pulpit that has mocked the messenger, the whole civic fabric that has made the false into the normal and the true into the offense. When the Qur'an says *how many a town*, it is naming the scale at which its history is written: the scale of the community, where the moral health of a place is a fact that can be seen from a distance, and where the punishment, when it comes, is the settlement of a communal account.

**The Hour of the Strike**

The verse then names the hour of the destruction, and the naming is one of the most pointed things in the Qur'an: *Our Might came upon them by night, or while they took their ease at midday*. The word for "Might" here is *ʿaẓāb* — punishment, and more than punishment, the punishment that displays a power: it is the word used for the divine strikes that the Qur'an recounts in its narrative suites, the wind that took a whole people in their beds, the flood that rose over the high places, the earthquake that overthrew a city with its dwellings. And the word "came upon them" (*jāʾahum*) is the word of the unexpected: it came *upon* them, not toward them, not as a thing they had arranged for — it fell on them.

The two hours named are the two hours in which a people is furthest from readiness. By night: the hour of rest, the hour when the senses are closed, the hour when no one is watching and no one is on guard. At midday, *while they took their ease* — the Arabic word is *ghināʾ* — the hour of play, of diversion, of a people that has settled into the rhythm of its own comfort and is attending to its own pleasures. The logic of the two hours is the same: it is in the hour of maximum relaxation that the heart is farthest from the awareness of God, and it is in that hour that the account is closed. The Qur'an's teaching is not that God strikes the sleeping or the playing man as a cruelty; it is that the man who has rejected the warning has already chosen the hour of his own account. He has spent his days in the ease that the verse names, and the ease is the opposite of the readiness for which the warning was sent. When the warning is rejected, the man has, in effect, said: I will not be ready. And the destruction, when it comes, comes at the hour that is the true shape of his choice: the hour in which he is not ready.

The Qur'an's own narratives keep the two hours in view. The punishment of Lot's people is spoken of as the striking of the towns *in the night* (15:65); the punishment of the people of Shuayb is the one that "came to them in the morning" (76:6, 46:26); the flood of Noah rose in the hour of their deepest security. The pattern is constant and it is the pattern of this verse: the destruction does not come with a second notice, and it does not come at a convenient hour. It comes when the people is at rest, because the people that has rejected the messenger has already made the rest its religion.

**The Warning of the Hour**

There is a law here that extends beyond the literal destruction of the towns, and the classical commentators drew it out. The law is this: once God has sent the warning and the people has refused it, the man is under no obligation to the man's comfort any longer. The comfort is the man's own, and the man has chosen to spend it in the rejection. The "ease at midday" is therefore not a description of God's method but a description of the man's state. The town that is destroyed at midday is the town that had been at ease at midday for years, and the midday of the destruction is only the last midday of a long noon of ease. The verse, applied to the individual life, is a warning about the hour in which a person feels most at ease with himself — the hour in which the career is secure, the health is good, the friends are pleased, and the question of the account has been pushed to the far side of the calendar. That hour is the hour the verse names, and the verse says the account can close in it, without notice, in the middle of the ease, in the very moment the man is furthest from the preparation. The practical consequence is not fear but readiness: the man who keeps the awareness of the account open does not sleep without the awareness, and he does not play without the awareness, and the destruction that "comes upon" the unready does not come upon him, because he is, in the Qur'an's word, the one who has been *prepared* — and the prepared man is not caught by the hour, in either world.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:5

> **Their plea, when Our Might came upon them, was but to say, “Truly we were wrongdoers.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Plea That Came Late**

The verse is short, and it is one of the most precise descriptions in the Qur'an of a moment that has happened to every destroyed town in the Book and that will happen, in a form, to every person who has refused the warning. The word for "plea" is *ʿajāh*, from the root *ʿ-j-h*, haste, urgency, the rush of a person who has seen the thing and is running toward it. The verse says that when the punishment came, the whole of their speech, the whole of their effort, the whole of the energy of their remaining life, was reduced to one sentence: *Truly we were wrongdoers.* The verse does not say they repented. It says they said. The difference is the whole of the verse, and the difference is the whole of the teaching.

**The Confession Without the Turning**

The word they said is *ẓālimūn*, "we were wrongdoers," from the root *ẓ-l-m*, and the word *ẓulm* in the Qur'an means, in its primary sense, the placing of a thing in a place other than its place — a misplacement, a putting out of joint. The man who is a *ẓālim* in the Qur'an's sense is the man who has put himself in the place that is not his place, who has taken the position of the one who answers for his own account when the account belongs to God, who has set his own will in the place of the will that is the true standard. The destroyed town, in its last hour, recognized the misplacement: *we were wrongdoers.* The recognition is real, and the recognition is late. The Qur'an's own distinction, drawn out in the commentators, is between the two kinds of confession. The first is the confession of the one who is still in the time in which the turning back is possible: the one who says "I have wronged myself" and then does the turning — the change of direction, the repair, the return. The second is the confession of the one for whom the door has closed: the one who says "I was a wrongdoer" in the tense of the past, in the hour in which the past is the only tense he has left. The verse is describing the second kind. The man in the verse is not the man who repents; he is the man who has been overtaken, and his confession is the confession of the condemned, which is real and which is not a remedy.

The Qur'an repeats this scene in several of the narrative suites, and the repetition is the teaching. The people of Noah, in the hour of the flood, are told of the same confession (11:40); the people of Lot's town are the ones who say, in their last hour, *Lord, forgive us* (26:109, 27:46); the people of Shuayb are the ones who, when the earthquake comes, say, *Lord, forgive us, for we were wrongdoers* (9:70, in the form of the warning); and the people of the Fire in the Hereafter are the ones whose whole speech is the same sentence in the past tense: *if only we had obeyed God and obeyed the messenger* (33:66, 47:21). The Qur'an's point, repeated across the Book, is that the confession without the turning is not a substitute for the turning; it is the last form of the refusal, dressed in the clothes of the recognition. The man who has spent his life in the ease of the midday and his nights in the rest is not saved in the last hour by the fact that he finally saw what he had been doing. The seeing was real; the hour was not.

**The Tense of the Confession**

There is a detail in the grammar of the verse that the commentators noted and that is worth stating. The confession is in the past tense: *we were wrongdoers*, not *we are wrongdoers.* The man who is in the hour of the destruction has, in the language of his own speech, placed himself in the past. He is no longer the wrongdoer; he is the one who was. The tense is the mark of the closed account: the man who is still in the time of the turning says *I am a wrongdoer, and I am turning*, and the tense is open; the man who is in the hour of the strike says *we were*, and the tense is closed. The Qur'an's use of the tenses is not a matter of grammar alone; it is a matter of the state of the account. The verse, in its four words of confession, is a description of the state in which the account has been closed, and the description is the warning of every hour in which the account is still open.

**The Contrast That Is the Cure**

The verse, read against the Qur'an's own teaching on repentance, becomes a map of the difference between the two kinds of confession, and the map is the practical part of the teaching. The Qur'an's own hadith — the one the Prophet ﷺ told about the servant who sins and then turns — is the direct opposite of the scene of this verse. In the hadith, the man who has sinned says, in the present, *O my Lord, I have done such-and-such, and I seek Your forgiveness*, and God says to the angels, *inform My servants that I have forgiven My servant, and he has turned to Me* — and the hadith continues with the detail that God's joy at the turning of His servant is greater than the joy of the man who has lost his camel in the desert and then found it. The two scenes are the two ends of the same road. The man in the verse of this sūrah is the man who waited until the hour of the destruction to say the sentence, and the sentence, in that hour, was the last word of a life. The man of the hadith is the man who said the sentence in the hour in which the sentence was still a remedy, and the sentence, in that hour, was the first word of a new life. The same sentence, in the two hours, is two different things: a confession that is too late, and a turning that is in time. The verse, then, is not only a description of the destroyed town; it is a clock. It is the hour at which the sentence stops being a cure and becomes a description, and the warning of the verse is that the man who is reading the verse is, at this moment, on the side of the clock on which the sentence is still a cure.

> *"Whoever among you sins and then repents, and finds that no door is open, let him seek the door of God; for God's door is open."* — The teaching of the sources on the standing of the door of repentance, and the hadith in the collections: *"The door of repentance remains open as long as the sun has not risen from the west."* (Abu Dawud, Ibn Majah)

The verse ends, and the next verse begins, and the next verse is the verse of the questioning — the verse that says the account will be opened, and the account will be read, and the account will be read with knowledge, and the One who reads it was never absent. The confession of this verse is the last word of the town; the questioning of the next verse is the first word of the hour in which the town will stand, in the full form of itself, in the presence of the One who was never absent.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:6

> **Then We shall surely question those unto whom Our message was sent, and We shall surely question the messengers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Who Stand Before the Question**

The verse names two groups who will stand before the question, and the order in which they are named is the order of the account. First: *those unto whom Our message was sent* — the people, the receivers, the ones who heard the warning and answered it as they answered it. Second: *the messengers* — the carriers, the ones who brought the message and delivered it. The double naming is one of the most complete statements in the Qur'an of the structure of the prophetic relation, and it settles at once the two questions that the structure raises. What is the messenger to be asked about? What is the people to be asked about? The verse answers both by the order: the people are asked first, because the account of the people is the account that is at issue; the messengers are asked second, because the account of the messengers is the account that closes the question of the people.

**What the Messengers Are Asked**

The messengers will be questioned, but the question for the messengers is a narrow one, and the Qur'an defines it elsewhere with precision. The duty of the messenger, in the Qur'an's own words, is the delivery of the message, and nothing more. The Prophet ﷺ is told, in several places, in the same short form: *the messengers are but conveyors* (16:35); *you are only a conveyor* (13:40, 35:27); *upon the messengers is but the clear conveyance* (16:82). The messenger is not responsible for the acceptance; the messenger is responsible for the delivery. The question, in the Hereafter, is the question of the delivery: *have you delivered?* And the answer of the messengers, in the Qur'an's own scenes, is the same answer in every case: the people of Lot's town are told, *the messengers have certainly come to you, conveying the clear signs* (43:7); the people of Noah's town are met with the same statement; the people of Pharaoh are told, *we have brought you the Book of your Lord, conveying the clear signs* (7:6, in the mouth of the messenger). The messenger's account is closed by the delivery, and the messenger's innocence is established by the fact that the delivery was done. The verse, in naming the messengers after the people, is saying: the messenger will stand, and the messenger will be asked, and the question of the messenger will be the question that the people cannot answer, because the people heard the delivery and chose the answer.

**What the People Are Asked**

The people are asked about their response, and the response is the whole of the account. The classical commentators drew out the distinction with care, because the Qur'an itself appears, in other verses, to say the opposite: that the guilty will not be questioned at all. The verse in al-Qasas says, *no man shall be questioned as to his sins* (28:78), in the context of the judgment of the people of the Fire; the verse in al-Rahman says, *no man shall be questioned as to his sins, nor shall any jinn* (55:39), in the context of the description of the Garden. The two sets of verses appear to contradict each other, and the later commentators resolved the apparent contradiction with a distinction that is worth stating in full. The people are not questioned about the sins as such — the sins are already known, and they are already recorded, and the recording is the book of deeds that each person will receive in the Hereafter (17:13, 69:19, 17:14). The question is not a question of information; God does not need to learn what the person has done. The question is a question of the reason: *why did you do this? What turned you away from the good? What was it that you chose, and why did you choose it?* The question is the question that the person cannot answer, because the person who has spent his life in the ease of the midday and his nights in the rest has no reason that he can produce in the hour of the account. The question, in other words, is not the court learning the facts; the question is the court making the man stand in front of the facts and produce the reason, and the man's inability to produce the reason is the man's own judgment.

The hadith that the sources gather for this verse states the scene in the form of the direct address:

> *"There is not one of you to whom his Lord will not speak without an interpreter between them on the Day of Resurrection. And He will say to him, 'Do you remember the day on which you did this and did that?' so that He may remind him of what he did in the world."* (Reported in Ahmad and al-Tirmidhī)

The hadith adds the detail that the Qur'an's verse implies: the question is not mediated. There is no interpreter, no advocate, no intercessor who stands between the man and the question. The man is addressed directly, by name, in the hour in which the man is the man, in the full form of himself, and the question is the question of the day in which the man did the thing. The scene is the scene of the verse, and the scene is the scene of the account.

**The Purpose of the Question**

The purpose of the question is not the information; the purpose is the rebuke. The classical commentators — the ones who drew out the purpose with the sharpest clarity — held that the question is the means by which the man is made to acknowledge his own fault, and to surrender the attempts to excuse the fault. The man who is in the ease of the midday has, in the ease, built the excuses: the excuse of the father, the excuse of the custom, the excuse of the friend, the excuse of the hour. The question, in the Hereafter, is the removal of the excuses, one by one, in the hour in which the excuses have no power. The man is asked, and the man has no answer, and the man's silence in the hour of the question is the man's own confession. The question is, in the Qur'an's psychology, the awakening of the conscience in the hour in which the conscience is fully awake, and the awakening is the judgment.

The logic of the whole structure is the logic of the court that has already seen everything. The court does not question the accused to learn the facts; the court questions the accused to make the accused stand in front of the facts. The court that has already seen the whole of the case, in the form in which the case was done, does not need the testimony of the accused; the court needs the confession of the accused. The verse, in its two lines, is the description of the court of the Hereafter: the One who questions has already seen, and the question is the removal of the man's self-deception, and the man's inability to answer is the man's own verdict.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:7

> **Then We shall recount unto them with knowledge, for We were never absent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recounting With Knowledge**

The verse moves from the question to the recounting, and the move is the move from the question that the man cannot answer to the reading of the account that the man cannot dispute. The word for "recount" is *naḥkī* — We shall tell, We shall recount, We shall set before them in the form of the story. The account is not a list; the account is a story, and the story is the man's own life, in the order in which the life was lived, in the form in which the acts were done. The detail that the verse adds to the recounting is the one that carries the weight: *with knowledge* (*bi-ʿilm*). The recounting is done with knowledge, and the knowledge is the knowledge that is complete: the knowledge of the inward and the outward, the public and the private, the act and the intention behind the act. The man who is in the ease of the midday has, in the ease, assumed that the private is safe, that the inward is hidden, that the act that was done in the room with the door closed is a thing that no one has seen. The verse corrects the assumption: the recounting is done with the knowledge that includes the room with the door closed, and the knowledge is the knowledge that was there in the hour of the act, not the knowledge that was reconstructed afterward.

The hadith that the sources gather for the verse states the form of the recounting in the way that makes the correction concrete:

> *"The slave's foot does not depart on the Day of Resurrection until he is questioned: about his life — in what did he spend it? About his youth — in what did he destroy it? About his knowledge — what did he do with it? About his wealth — from where did he acquire it, and in what did he spend it?"* (Ahmad, and others)

The hadith is the form of the recounting: the four questions of the life, in the order of the life, and each question is the question of the use — *in what did you spend it?* The recounting is not the reading of the acts; the recounting is the reading of the uses. The man is not asked what he did; the man is asked what he did the thing for, and what he made of the thing that was given to him. The four questions are the four parts of the human account, and the four questions are the four parts of the life that the man, in the ease, had assumed was his own.

**The One Who Was Never Absent**

The verse closes with the reason for the completeness of the recounting, and the reason is the most complete statement of God's presence in the Qur'an's own short form: *for We were never absent* (*ma kunnā ghāʾibīn*). The word *ghāʾib* means the one who is not present, the one who is away, the one who is not in the place. The verse says that God was never in the state of not being present: never away, never in another place, never in the hour in which the act was done, not in the room, not in the moment, not in the inward. The presence is not the presence of the witness who was in the room at the time; the presence is the presence that is the condition of the room, and the presence was there in the hour of the act, and the presence is the presence that makes the act an act, and the presence is the presence that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not there.

The Qur'an states the same presence in several places, in the form that makes the correction of the man's assumption concrete. The verse in al-Ḥadīd says:

> *"He is with you wherever you are. And God sees whatever you do."* (57:4)

The verse in al-Furqān says:

> *"And We are nearer to him than his jugular vein."* (50:16)

The two verses are the two forms of the same presence: the presence that is with the man in the place, and the presence that is nearer to the man than the man's own self. The presence is not a presence that the man can escape by leaving the place; the presence is a presence that is the condition of the man's being in the place, and the presence is the presence that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not there.

**The Two Attributes in One Verse**

The verse, in its two lines, names two of God's attributes in the form that is the foundation of the account. The first line — the recounting *with knowledge* — is the statement of the knowledge that is complete: the attribute of the One who knows the inward and the outward, the public and the private, the act and the intention. The second line — *We were never absent* — is the statement of the presence that is complete: the attribute of the One who is with the man in the hour of the act, and who is nearer to the man than the man's own self. The two attributes are the two conditions of the account that is complete: the account is complete because the One who reads it knows the whole of it, and the account is complete because the One who reads it was there in the hour of the act. The two lines, together, are the foundation of the whole structure of the Hereafter in the Qur'an: the account is not a reconstruction; the account is the disclosure of what was already recorded, and the recording was done by the One who was present in the hour of the act.

**The Practical Consequence**

The practical consequence of the verse is the one that the classical commentators drew out with the sharpest clarity, and it is the consequence that the Qur'an's own teaching on the inner life is built upon. The man who knows that the One who is present is present in the hour of the act, and who knows that the presence is the presence that was there in the room with the door closed, is the man who acts in the hour of the act as the one who is seen. The Qur'an's own definition of the highest form of the worship states the condition in the hadith that the Prophet ﷺ told to his companions:

> *"Ihsan is that you worship God as though you see Him. And if you do not see Him, then He surely sees you."* (Bukhari)

The hadith is the form of the verse: the presence that is the condition of the act, and the act that is done in the presence. The man who acts in the presence is the man whose account is not a surprise; the man's account is the disclosure of the man's own life, in the form in which the life was lived, and the man, in the hour of the disclosure, has no excuse, because the man, in the hour of the act, had assumed that the presence was not there. The verse, in its two lines, is the correction of the assumption, and the correction is the foundation of the inner life: the man who lives in the presence is the man whose account is not a judgment but a recognition, and the recognition is the recognition of the man who has lived in the form in which the man will stand.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:8

> **And the weighing on that Day is true. So those whose balance is heavy, it is they who shall prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Image of the Balance**

The verse moves from the question to the weighing, and it does so with the image that the Qur'an returns to again and again for the description of the final judgment: the balance, the *mīzān*, the scales. The weighing on that Day is described in one word — *ṣadīq*, true — and the word carries the double sense that the Arabic gives it: the weighing is true in that it is just, and it is true in that it is reliable, the weighing that does not deceive, that does not err, that does not favor. The balance is the image of the judgment that is not the judgment of the powerful over the weak, but the judgment of the standard over the man, and the standard is the same for all. The Qur'an states the same image in several places, and the repetition is the teaching. The verse in al-Anbiyāʾ says:

> *"And We place the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul shall be treated unjustly in the least. And even if it be the weight of a mustard seed, We bring it. And sufficient are We as reckoners."* (21:47)

The verse in al-Zukhruf and the verse in al-Qiyāma repeat the same image in the same short form: the weighing is the weighing, and the one whose deeds are heavy is the one who prospers, and the one whose deeds are light is the one who has lost. The image is the image of the judgment that is not a matter of opinion, and the image is the image of the judgment that is a matter of measure.

**The Weight of the Deed**

The verse does not say that the deeds are weighed in the form of the size; the verse says that the deeds are weighed, and the weight of the deed is not the size of the deed. The Qur'an's own teaching on the weight of the deed is the teaching of the intention: the Prophet ﷺ said, in the hadith that opens the collections,

> *"Verily deeds are judged by intentions, and every man shall have what he intended."* (Bukhari, Muslim)

The weight of the deed is the sincerity in the deed, and the sincerity is the quality that makes the small deed heavy and the large deed light. The hadith of the three men in the cave, the one that Bukhari and Muslim gathered, is the form of the teaching: the three men are trapped in the cave, and the cave is closed, and the three men each tell of the deed that he did for the sake of God, and the deed of each of them is small in the form of the size — the man who gave the cows to the widow, the man who did not take the money of the orphan, the man who did the thing with his own sister that he did not do — and the deed of each of them is accepted, and the cave is opened, because the weight of the deed was the sincerity in the deed, and the sincerity was the weight that the balance measured. The verse of this sūrah is the form of the teaching: the balance is the balance that measures the sincerity, and the sincerity is the weight that the balance measures.

**The Balance of the Cosmos and the Balance of the Market**

The image of the balance in the Qur'an is not a single image; the image is the image that appears in three places, and the three places are the three levels of the same principle. The first is the balance of the cosmos: the Qur'an says that God has made the *mīzān* — the balance — for all things, and that the heavens and the earth and what is between them are made in the balance (55:7–9, 57:25). The second is the balance of the market: the Qur'an commands the believers to "establish weight in justice, and do not make deficient the balance" (17:35, 26:181–183, 55:8–9). The third is the balance of the Hereafter: the weighing of the deeds in the final judgment. The three levels are the three levels of the same principle: the principle that all things are made in the balance, and the balance is the standard by which the thing is measured, and the balance is the standard that the man, in the market and in the life and in the Hereafter, is measured by. The verse of this sūrah is the third level of the principle, and the third level is the level that closes the account.

The connection between the three levels is the connection that the classical commentators drew out with care. The man who establishes the balance in the market is the man who is establishing the balance in the life, and the man who is establishing the balance in the life is the man whose deeds are heavy in the balance of the Hereafter. The man who makes deficient the balance in the market is the man who is making deficient the balance in the life, and the man who is making deficient the balance in the life is the man whose deeds are light in the balance of the Hereafter. The three levels are the three levels of the same act, and the act is the act of the man who measures, and the man who measures is the man who is measured.

**The Hadith of the Heaviest**

The hadith that the sources gather for the verse states the weight of the deed in the form that makes the teaching concrete:

> *"Nothing is heavier in the balance than good character (ḥusn al-khuluq)."* (Al-Tirmidhī)

The hadith is the form of the verse: the heaviest thing in the balance is not the large act of worship, but the quality of the character, and the quality of the character is the quality that makes the deed heavy. The hadith is the teaching of the verse, and the teaching is the teaching that the man who is measured by the balance is the man who is measured by the quality of the character, and the quality of the character is the quality that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not measured.

**The Prosperity of the Heavy**

The verse closes with the result of the weighing: *those whose balance is heavy, it is they who shall prosper*. The word is *al-mufridūn*, from the root *f-z-l*, excess, prosperity, the state of the one who has more than he needs. The prosperity of the heavy balance is not the prosperity of the wealth, but the prosperity of the soul: the soul that has more than it needs, the soul that is in the state of the one who has been given, and the giving is the giving of the Garden, and the Garden is the state of the one whose balance is heavy. The verse, in its two lines, is the description of the judgment that is the judgment of the standard, and the standard is the standard that is the same for all, and the man who is measured by the standard is the man who is measured by the quality of the sincerity, and the sincerity is the weight that the balance measures.

The practical consequence of the verse is the one that the classical commentators drew out with the sharpest clarity: the man who is measured by the balance is the man who measures himself, and the measuring of the self is the habit that the Prophet ﷺ commanded his companions to keep. The hadith that the sources gather states the command in the short form:

> *"O you who have believed in God, count yourselves before you are counted, and weigh yourselves before you are weighed."* (Reported in the collections of the later authorities)

The command is the form of the verse: the man who weighs himself in the life is the man whose weighing in the Hereafter is not a surprise, and the weighing of the self is the weighing that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not necessary. The verse, in its two lines, is the invitation to the weighing of the self, and the invitation is the invitation that the man, in the hour of the account, is not invited to, because the man, in the hour of the account, is already weighed.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:9

> **And as for those whose balance is light, it is they who have ruined their souls by having treated Our signs wrongfully.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lightness That Is a Choice**

The verse is the second half of the weighing, and it names the state of the one whose balance is light. The word for "light" is *khuffā*, the lightness that is the opposite of the heaviness, and the lightness is not a misfortune that has happened to the man, but the state that the man has made for himself. The verse gives the reason for the lightness, and the reason is the one that the Qur'an gives in the same form in several places: the man whose balance is light is the man who has treated the signs of God wrongfully. The word for "wrongfully" is *ẓalimūna*, from the root *ẓ-l-m*, the misplacement, and the misplacement here is the misplacement of the signs: the man who has taken the signs of God — the signs in the Book, the signs in the cosmos, the signs in the history, the signs in the self — and has put them in the place that is not their place: the place of the thing that is not to be believed, the place of the thing that is to be mocked, the place of the thing that is to be ignored. The lightness of the balance is the result of the misplacement of the signs, and the misplacement is the act of the man, and the act is the act that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not an act.

**The Signs as the Weight**

The logic of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full, because it is the logic that holds the whole teaching of the Qur'an on the relation between the signs and the deeds. The signs of God are the substance by which the deed gains its weight. The deed that is done in the light of the sign is the deed that has the weight of the sign in it, and the deed that is done in the darkness of the rejection of the sign is the deed that has lost the weight of the sign. The man who rejects the sign is the man who has removed the substance by which the deed is heavy, and the man who has removed the substance is the man whose balance is light, because the balance is the balance that measures the substance, and the substance is the substance that the man has rejected. The verse, in its short form, is the statement of the law: the response of the man to the signs is the weight of the man's balance, and the man who treats the signs wrongfully is the man whose balance is light, because the man has removed the substance by which the deed is heavy.

The Qur'an states the same law in the form of the description of the state of the heart of the man who rejects the signs. The verse in al-Anʿām says:

> *"So when they forgot that of which they had been reminded, We opened for them every door. And until, when they rejoiced in what they were doing, We seized them suddenly. And they were in despair."* (6:44)

And the verse in the same sūrah says:

> *"And when they turned away, We turned away. And the eyes of the disbelievers were darkened."* (6:46, in the form of the sealing of the heart)

The two verses are the form of the law: the man who rejects the sign is the man whose heart is sealed, and the man whose heart is sealed is the man whose balance is light, because the heart that is sealed is the heart that has lost the connection to the substance by which the deed is heavy. The verse of this sūrah is the statement of the result, and the result is the result of the act of the man.

**The Ruin of the Soul**

The verse names the loss of the light balance, and the loss is not the loss of the wealth or the status or the life; the loss is the loss of the soul. The word is *al-ḥāirūn*, from the root *ḥ-w-r*, the state of the one who is in loss, the one who has been ruined, the one who has lost the thing that is the thing. The soul, in the Qur'an's own usage, is the thing that is the man: the man is his soul, and the loss of the soul is the loss of the man. The verse, in naming the loss of the soul as the loss of the light balance, is stating the principle that the Qur'an states in the same form in several places: the greatest loss is the loss of the self, and the loss of the self is the loss that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not possible.

The hadith that the sources gather states the principle in the form of the warning:

> *"The greatest loss of a man is the loss of his soul."* — The teaching of the sources, in the form of the hadith that the Prophet ﷺ told to his companions, in the context of the description of the state of the man who has lost the soul in the Hereafter.

The hadith is the form of the verse: the loss of the soul is the loss that is the loss, and the loss of the soul is the loss that the man, in the hour of the account, is not able to recover.

**The Wrongful Treatment of the Signs**

The verse names the act that produces the lightness, and the act is the act of the man who has treated the signs wrongfully. The act is not the act of the ignorance; the act is the act of the choice. The man who does not know the sign is not the man who has treated the sign wrongfully; the man who has seen the sign, and has understood the sign, and has chosen the rejection of the sign, is the man who has treated the sign wrongfully. The distinction is the distinction that the Qur'an draws in the same form in several places: the man who is in the state of the ignorance is the man to whom the sign has not reached, and the man who is in the state of the rejection is the man to whom the sign has reached, and who has chosen the rejection. The verse of this sūrah is the description of the second kind, and the second kind is the kind that the Qur'an describes in the form of the warning.

The practical consequence of the verse is the one that is the consequence of the whole teaching: the man who is measured by the balance is the man who is measured by his response to the signs, and the response to the signs is the act of the man, and the act of the man is the act that the man, in the hour of the ease, is able to do. The man who is reading the verse is the man who is in the hour of the ease, and the man who is in the hour of the ease is the man who is able to respond to the sign in the form that makes the deed heavy. The verse, in its two lines, is the warning and the invitation: the warning is the warning of the lightness, and the invitation is the invitation to the heaviness, and the heaviness is the heaviness that the man, in the hour of the ease, is able to make for himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:10

> **And We have indeed established you upon the earth and placed means of livelihood for you therein. Little do you give thanks!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Favors Before the Story**

The verse is short, and it stands at the gate of the longest narrative in the sūrah — the story of Adam, which begins in the next verse and runs through verse twenty-eight. The verse does something that the classical commentators noted with care: it names the two favors that every human being has, and that are the condition of the story that follows, before the story is told. The first favor is the establishment: *We have indeed established you upon the earth*. The second favor is the sustenance: *We have placed means of livelihood for you therein*. And the verse closes with the rebuke that names the response that the two favors require: *Little do you give thanks!* The verse is the introduction of the story of Adam, and the introduction is the introduction that says: before you hear the story of the first man, know the state of the men who are hearing the story. The men who are hearing the story are the men who are established upon the earth, and who have the means of livelihood in the earth, and who have, in the state, forgotten the One who established them and placed the means.

**The Word for Establishment**

The word for "established" is *makkannā*, from the root *m-k-n*, and the root carries the sense of the settled place, the standing position, the state of the one who has a place that is not to be moved. The word *makan* means the place, and the state of the one who is *makīn* is the state of the one who has the place, and the state is the state of the stability. The verse, in naming the establishment of the humanity upon the earth, is naming the state of the humanity in the form that the Qur'an names in the same short form in the verse of the khilāfa: *I am going to place upon the earth a vicegerent* (2:30). The two verses are the two forms of the same statement: the humanity is placed upon the earth, and the placing is the placing of the one who is given the place, and the place is the place that is the condition of the responsibility. The establishment is not the state of the one who is a guest; the establishment is the state of the one who is given the place, and the place is the place that the man is responsible for.

The verse, in its form, is the form of the introduction of the story of Adam: the story of Adam is the story of the man who was established in the Garden, and who was taken from the Garden, and who was established upon the earth. The two verses of the Qur'an that close the two accounts of the story of Adam (2:36, 7:24) both name the establishment upon the earth as the result of the descent: the man who was in the Garden is the man who is upon the earth, and the earth is the place of the dwelling, and the dwelling is the state of the man who is established. The verse of this sūrah is the form of the introduction, and the introduction is the form that says: the story of Adam is the story of the man who is established upon the earth, and the man who is established upon the earth is the man who is hearing the story.

**The Means of Livelihood**

The second favor is the favor of the means of livelihood, and the word for the means is *rizq*, from the root *r-z-q*, and the root carries the sense of the provision, the thing that is given to the man to live. The verse says that God has placed the means of livelihood in the earth: the earth is the source of the means, and the means are in the earth, and the earth is the earth that God has made the source. The logic of the statement is the logic that the Qur'an states in the same form in several places: the means of the man are in the earth, and the earth is the earth that God has established, and the establishment of the earth is the establishment of the source of the means. The man who is established upon the earth is the man who has the means in the earth, and the means are the means that God has placed, and the placing is the act of God, and the act of God is the act that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not an act.

The verse, in its two favors, is the form of the statement of the state of the humanity: the humanity is established upon the earth, and the humanity has the means in the earth, and the state is the state that the man, in the ease, had assumed was the state of the man, and the state is the state that the man, in the ease, had assumed was not the state of the One who established.

**The Rebuke of the Ingratitude**

The verse closes with the rebuke, and the rebuke is the rebuke that names the response that the two favors require: *Little do you give thanks!* The word for "give thanks" is *tashkurūn*, from the root *s-h-r*, and the root carries the sense of the recognition of the favor, and the declaration of the favor, and the use of the favor in the way that the favor is to be used. The three forms of the thanksgiving are the three forms that the classical commentators drew out: the thanksgiving of the heart, which is the recognition of the favor; the thanksgiving of the tongue, which is the declaration of the favor; and the thanksgiving of the limbs, which is the use of the favor in the way that the favor is to be used. The man who is grateful in the three forms is the man who is grateful in the form that the verse requires, and the man who is not grateful in the three forms is the man who is not grateful in the form that the verse requires.

The Qur'an states the law of the thanksgiving in the form of the promise:

> *"If you are grateful, I will surely increase you. And if you deny, verily My punishment is severe."* (14:7)

The promise is the form of the verse: the man who is grateful is the man who is increased, and the increase is the increase of the favor, and the favor is the favor that the man, in the ease, had assumed was the favor of the man, and the favor is the favor that the One who established gives.

The practical consequence of the verse is the one that is the consequence of the whole teaching: the man who is established upon the earth is the man who has the means in the earth, and the man who has the means in the earth is the man who is able to give thanks, and the giving of thanks is the act that the man, in the hour of the ease, is able to do. The verse, in its two favors and its one rebuke, is the introduction of the story of Adam, and the introduction is the form that says: the story of Adam is the story of the man who was established in the Garden, and who forgot the One who established him, and who was taken from the Garden, and who was established upon the earth, and who was, in the earth, reminded of the One who established him.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:11

> **Indeed, We created you, then We formed you, then We said unto the angels, “Prostrate yourselves before Adam.” And they all prostrated, save Iblīs; he was not among those who prostrated.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Account of the First Man**

The verse opens the account of the creation of Adam, and the account is the second account in the order of the Qur'an: the first account is in Sūrah al-Baqarah (2:30–39), and the later accounts are in Sūrah al-Isrāʾ (17:61–65) and Sūrah Tā-Hā (20:115–24), with the similar accounts in Sūrah al-Hijr (15:28–43) and Sūrah Ṣād (38:71–85) that tell the same story without the specific naming of Adam. The repetition of the story in the Qur'an is not a repetition of the information; the repetition is the repetition of the teaching, and the teaching is the teaching that the story of the first man is the story of every man, and the story of every man is the story that the Qur'an tells again and again, in the form that makes the teaching concrete for the man who is hearing the story in the hour of the hearing.

**The Creation and the Formation**

The verse begins with the double statement of the creation: *We created you, then We formed you*. The two words are the two stages of the creation of the man: the creation, which is the making of the man from the substance, and the formation, which is the shaping of the man in the form. The root of the first word is *kh-l-ṣ*, the making of the thing that was not, and the root of the second word is *ṣ-w-w*, the shaping of the thing in the form. The Qur'an states the two stages in the form of the description of the embryo:

> *"We created man from a clinging substance, We placed him in a settled place. Then We made the clinging substance into a clot, and We made the clot into a lump, and We made the lump into bones, and We covered the bones with flesh. Then We originated him as another creation. Blessed is God, the best of creators."* (23:12–14)

The two stages are the two stages of the creation of the man, and the two stages are the two stages that the verse names in the short form. The address of the verse is the address to the humanity in the plural: *We created you* — the "you" is the humanity, and the humanity is the humanity that was created in the form of the seed of the loins of Adam. The verse, in its form, is the form that says: the story of Adam is the story of the humanity, and the humanity is the humanity that was created in Adam, and the creation of the humanity in Adam is the creation that the verse names.

The verse of this sūrah, in the form of the address to the humanity, is connected to the verse of the covenant that the Qur'an states in the same sūrah, a few verses later (7:172):

> *"And when your Lord took from the Children of Adam, from their loins, their progeny, and made them bear witness concerning themselves: Am I not your Lord? They said: Yes, we bear witness."* (7:172)

The two verses are the two forms of the same statement: the humanity was created in Adam, and the humanity was made to bear witness to the Lordship of God in the hour of the creation, and the bearing of the witness is the bearing of the witness that the man, in the hour of the account, is not able to deny. The verse of the creation and the verse of the covenant are the two forms of the same teaching: the man is the man who was created in Adam, and the man who was created in Adam is the man who is the man who is hearing the story of Adam, and the story of Adam is the story of the man.

**The Command of the Prostration**

The verse then states the command that is the command of the story: *We said unto the angels: Prostrate yourselves before Adam*. The word for "prostrate" is *sajadū*, from the root *s-j-d*, and the root carries the sense of the most complete form of the humility: the man who prostrates is the man who places the most noble part of his body, the forehead, upon the ground, and the placing of the forehead upon the ground is the placing of the man in the form of the one who is in the state of the complete submission. The command of the prostration before Adam is the command that the angels were to obey, and the obedience of the command is the obedience that is the test of the state of the angels.

The commentators drew out the meaning of the command with care, because the command appears to be the command that is not the command of the worship: the prostration is the form of the worship, and the worship is the worship of God, and the prostration before Adam is not the prostration of the worship of Adam. The resolution that the classical commentators gave is the resolution that is worth stating in full. The prostration before Adam is the prostration of the honor, not the prostration of the worship: the angels prostrated before Adam as the form of the recognition of the station of Adam, and the station of Adam is the station that God gave to Adam, and the recognition of the station is the recognition that is the obedience to the command of God. The prostration is the prostration in which the angels take Adam as the direction, and the direction of the prostration is God, and the prostration is the prostration that is the form of the recognition of the order of the creation. The verse in Yūsuf states the same form of the prostration in the form of the family of Yūsuf:

> *"And his parents and his family prostrated to him."* (12:100)

The prostration of the family of Yūsuf before Yūsuf is the prostration of the honor, and the prostration is the prostration that is the form of the recognition of the station of Yūsuf, and the station of Yūsuf is the station that God gave to Yūsuf. The two prostrations are the two forms of the same teaching: the prostration before the created is the prostration of the honor, and the prostration of the honor is the prostration that is the obedience to the order of God.

**The Refusal of Iblīs**

The verse closes with the statement of the refusal: *And they all prostrated, save Iblīs; he was not among those who prostrated*. The grammar of the statement is the grammar that the commentators noted: the verse says that Iblīs was not among those who prostrated, and the statement implies that Iblīs was among the angels, because the "those who prostrated" are the angels, and Iblīs is not among the angels who prostrated. The verse in al-Kahf (18:50) states, in the same context, that Iblīs is of the jinn:

> *"Except Iblīs. He was of the jinn, and he defied the command of his Lord, becoming ungrateful."* (18:50)

The two verses give rise to the question that the scholars discussed: was Iblīs an angel, or was Iblīs a jinn? The evidence of the Qur'an is the evidence that the two verses give, and the two verses are the two forms of the statement, and the statement is the statement that Iblīs was among the angels in the state of the prostration, and Iblīs is of the jinn in the state of the substance. The resolution that the later commentators gave is the resolution that holds the two verses together: Iblīs was, in the state of the prostration, among the angels, and the state of the prostration is the state of the proximity to God, and the jinn and the angels are the two creations that are near to God, and Iblīs was, in the state of the proximity, among the angels, and in the state of the substance, of the jinn. The question is the question that the scholars discussed, and the discussion is the discussion that the later commentators carried out with care, and the care is the care that is the form of the reverence for the text.

The refusal of Iblīs is the refusal that is the heart of the story, and the refusal is the refusal that the next verse will state in the form of the argument of Iblīs, and the argument of Iblīs is the argument that the sūrah will carry through the whole of the account.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:12

> **He said, “What prevented thee from prostrating when I commanded thee?” He said, “I am better than he. Thou hast created me from fire, while Thou hast created him from clay.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Is the Rebuke**

The verse opens with the question of God to Iblīs, and the question is the question that the classical commentators recognized as the question of the rebuke, not the question of the inquiry. The word is *manʿaka* — "what prevented thee?" — and the question is the question that is the form of the verse of the questioning of this sūrah (7:6): the One who questions has already seen, and the question is the question that makes the one who is questioned state his reason, and the reason is the reason that is the condemnation. Iblīs, in the hour of the question, has the opportunity to state the reason, and the reason that Iblīs states is the reason that is the whole of the fall: the claim of the superiority, and the argument of the element.

**The Claim That Is the Sin**

The first word of the answer of Iblīs is the word that is the heart of the answer: *I am better than he* (*ana afdal minhu*). The root is *f-d-l*, excess, superiority, and the claim is the claim of the superiority of Iblīs over Adam.
