## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:116

> **He said, “Cast!” And when they cast, they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe, and they brought forth a mighty sorcery.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Casting of the Sorcerers**

The verse states the reply of Moses to the demand of the sorcerers, and the reply is the reply that is the graciousness of Moses: *He said, "Cast!"* Moses responds graciously by letting the sorcerers cast first, both because he understands their desire to do so and because he is certain that God will aid him, and that no sorcery can defeat the miracle, which has a divine cause and is inimitable through mere human agency. The reply of Moses is the reply that is the confidence of the prophet in the aid of God, and the confidence of the prophet in the aid of God is the confidence that is the teaching that the miracle of God is the miracle that is the thing that cannot be defeated by the sorcery of the people. The sorcery that allows the sorcerers to cast first also allows Moses to demonstrate more easily the powerlessness of their deceptions in the face of the truth, since the power of his own staff is then able to devour all their deceptions.

**The Deception and the Awe**

The verse states the act of the sorcerers when they cast, and the act is the act that is the bewitching of the eyes of the people and the striking of them with awe: *And when they cast, they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe, and they brought forth a mighty sorcery*. The sorcerers produced the tricks that bewitched the eyes of the people through the slight of hand and the illusion, and the tricks of the sorcerers are the tricks that are the casting of the ropes and the wooden staffs, which were painted so as to create the illusion of the writhing snakes filling the ground, one upon another. The other reports indicate that the sorcerers had placed the mercury inside the staffs and the ropes so that they began to move in the hot sun, and the competition was held in the open desert at the hottest time of the day. The people were struck with awe by the feats of the sorcerers, and the awe of the people is the awe that is the state of the people who are struck by the display of the sorcerers. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are struck with awe by the feats of the sorcerers are the people who are in the ones who are struck by the illusion, and the people who are struck by the illusion are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the test of the truth and the falsehood, and the test of the truth and the falsehood is the test that is the distinction between the sorcery and the miracle. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the casting of the sorcerers, and the statement of the casting of the sorcerers is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the casting of the staff of Moses in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:117

> **And We revealed unto Moses, “Cast thy staff!” And, behold, it devoured all their deceptions.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of the Casting**

The verse states the command of God to Moses, and the command is the command that is the direct instruction: *And We revealed unto Moses, "Cast thy staff!"* Although Moses was expected to cast his staff to produce the great feat, he waits until God reveals unto him the direct instruction to cast it, and the command was heard only by Moses. The waiting of Moses for the command of God is the waiting that is the teaching that the power and the will behind this feat belong to God alone, and the power and the will that belong to God alone are the power and the will that are the teaching that is on the form of the other verses (3:49; 5:110), in which the miracles of Jesus are explicitly performed by the leave of God. The logic of the waiting of Moses is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who waits for the command of God before the act is the prophet who is in the one who demonstrates the dependence of the prophet upon God, and the dependence of the prophet upon God is the dependence that is the teaching that the sign of the prophet is the sign that is the sign of God in the hand of the prophet.

**The Devouring of the Deceptions**

The verse states the result of the casting of the staff of Moses, and the result is the result that is the devouring of the deceptions: *And, behold, it devoured all their deceptions*. The staff of Moses becomes a serpent, as it did before Pharaoh alone in verse 107, and this serpent is reported to have literally devoured the deceptions of the sorcerers, that is, to have swallowed up the staffs and the ropes that had been made to look like the writhing serpents. When Moses grasped the staff again, it returned to its previous form, as it did when God first instructed him in this miraculous sign in the holy valley of Ṭuwā. The logic of the devouring of the deceptions is the logic that is worth stating: the serpent of Moses is the serpent that devours the deceptions of the sorcerers, and the serpent that devours the deceptions is the serpent that is the truth that devours the falsehood, on the form of the teaching of the next verse. The staff of Moses that returns to its previous form after the devouring of the deceptions is the staff that is the teaching that the sign of God is the sign that is the sign that returns to the hand of the prophet the completion of the sign. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the casting of the staff of Moses, and the statement of the casting of the staff of Moses is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the truth in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:118

> **Thus the truth came to pass, and whatsoever they did was shown to be false.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Coming to Pass of the Truth**

The verse states the result of the competition, and the result is the result that is the coming to pass of the truth: *Thus the truth came to pass, and whatsoever they did was shown to be false*. The word for "came to pass" is the word that can also mean to "fall" or to "alight," and some have suggested that here it means that the truth alighted upon the hearts of the sorcerers, for in the following verses they submit themselves to God and Moses. The statement that the truth came to pass is the statement that is the becoming plain and the becoming victorious of the truth over the deceptions of the sorcerers, and the becoming plain and the becoming victorious of the truth is the becoming that is the validation of the prophethood of Moses.

**The Juxtaposition of the Truth and the Falsehood**

The statement of the verse is similar to the other statements that juxtapose the truth and the falsehood in order to demonstrate the invincibility of the truth. The other statements are on the form of the verse in which the Prophet ﷺ is commanded to say that the truth has come, and the falsehood has vanished, and truly falsehood is ever vanishing (17:81), and the verse that states that God casts the truth against the falsehood, and it crushes it, and behold, it vanishes (21:18), and the verse that states that God wipes out the falsehood and verifies the truth through His words (42:24). The four statements are the four forms of the same teaching: the truth is the truth that is the invincible thing, and the falsehood is the falsehood that is the vanishing thing, and the truth that is the invincible thing and the falsehood that is the vanishing thing are the two things that are the teaching that the truth of God is the truth that is the thing that comes to pass, and the falsehood of the people is the falsehood that is the thing that is shown to be false. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the result of the competition, and the statement of the result of the competition is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the submission of the sorcerers in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:119

> **Then and there they were vanquished and turned back, humbled.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Vanquishing and the Turning Back**

The verse states the act of Pharaoh and the sorcerers after the casting of the staff of Moses, and the act is the act that is the vanquishing and the turning back: *Then and there they were vanquished and turned back, humbled*. The "they" of the verse is the both Pharaoh and the sorcerers, and the two groups are the two groups that are the vanquishing by the feat of Moses, and the vanquishing of the two groups is the vanquishing that is the result of the competition. Pharaoh reportedly released Moses from the detainment or the imprisonment, after the competition. The logic of the turning back of the sorcerers is the logic that is worth stating in full: the sorcerers who are vanquished are the sorcerers who are in the ones who turn back, and the turning back of the sorcerers is the turning back that is the immediate change of heart of the sorcerers, and the immediate change of heart of the sorcerers is the change that is the state of the sorcerers who are humbled and amazed before the awesome power displayed by Moses. The sorcerers who turn back are the sorcerers who are in the ones who know that their own feats were the mere deception, and the sorcerers who know that their own feats were the mere deception are the sorcerers who are in the ones who recognize that the feat produced by Moses could only have been of the divine provenance. The man who knows that his own art is the deception is the man who is in the one who recognizes the truth when the truth is displayed before him, and the man who recognizes the truth when the truth is displayed before him is the man who is in the one who is the object of the guidance of God. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the vanquishing and the turning back, and the statement of the vanquishing and the turning back is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the prostration of the sorcerers in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:120

> **And the sorcerers were cast down prostrate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prostration of the Sorcerers**

The verse states the act of the sorcerers, and the act is the act that is the prostration: *And the sorcerers were cast down prostrate*. The word for "were cast down prostrate" is the same word that was used in the preceding verses to denote the action of the casting down of the staffs, and the identity of the word indicates the speed and the force with which the sorcerers prostrated themselves, as if they were literally "cast down," involuntarily, by what they had observed. The use of the same word also sharpens the contrast between their earlier "casting" of the staffs for the purposes of the deception and their now "being cast" into the sincere submission to God. The sorcerers made themselves prostrate, in the display of the utter humility and the submission to God.

**The Spiritual Value of the Knowledge**

The commentary of al-Rāzī states that the immediate realization of the master sorcerers of the divine provenance of the feat of Moses, and their subsequent submission to God and Moses, is an indication of the spiritual value of the knowledge, for it was the sorcerers' advanced knowledge of their own art that allowed them to recognize that the actions of Moses were not the sorcery at all. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the knowledge of the art is the knowledge that is the recognition of the limits of the art, and the recognition of the limits of the art is the recognition that is the understanding of the inadequacy of the art in relation to the spiritual realities and the higher principles. The full knowledge even of the worldly sciences should also engender the understanding of the limits and the inadequacies of those sciences in relation to the sciences based upon the spiritual realities and the higher principles, particularly when one is directly confronted with the latter. For this reason, the Islamic tradition argues that each messenger was sent with the miracles that demonstrated the inimitable mastery of the leading arts and the sciences of the people to whom they had been sent. Moses is thus sent with the miracles that are similar to, but that utterly transcend, those of the sorcerers of his day, in a society where the sorcery and the occult sciences seem to have been held in the great esteem. Jesus was sent with the healing miracles in an age when the medical science enjoyed the great prestige. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was sent to the Arabs, whose major art form was the poetry and the eloquent speech, with the scripture whose literary and linguistic style was considered to be of the unsurpassed beauty and power. The three cases are the three forms of the same teaching: the miracle of the messenger is the miracle that is the mastery that transcends the art of the people to whom the messenger was sent, and the mastery that transcends the art of the people is the mastery that is the proof of the prophethood of the messenger. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the prostration of the sorcerers, and the statement of the prostration of the sorcerers is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the confession of the faith of the sorcerers in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:121

> **They said, “We believe in the Lord of the worlds,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confession of the Sorcerers**

The verse states the confession of the faith of the sorcerers, and the confession is the confession that is the belief in the Lord of the worlds: *We believe in the Lord of the worlds*. The title "the Lord of the worlds" is the title for God that is found throughout the Qur'an, beginning with the opening sūrah (1:2), and the title is invoked several times in this sūrah (7:54; 7:61; 7:67; 7:104). The logic of the confession is the logic that is worth stating in full: the sorcerers who confess the belief in the Lord of the worlds are the sorcerers who are in the ones who turn from the worship of the art of the sorcery to the worship of the Lord of the worlds, and the sorcerers who turn from the worship of the art of the sorcery to the worship of the Lord of the worlds are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the objects of the guidance of God. The confession of the faith of the sorcerers is the confession that is the reversal of the state of the sorcerers: the sorcerers who were the objects of the competition are the sorcerers who become the believers, and the sorcerers who become the believers are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the examples of the teaching of the distinction between the sorcery and the miracle, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 111. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the sorcerers who believe are the sorcerers who are in the ones who were inwardly disposed to the belief, and the sorcerers who were inwardly disposed to the belief are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the objects of the encouragement of the belief by the miracle of God, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 111. The verse, in its form, is the form of the first part of the confession of the sorcerers, and the first part of the confession is the part that is in the form of the introduction of the second part of the confession in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:122

> **the Lord of Moses and Aaron.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lord of Moses and Aaron**

The verse is the second part of the confession of the sorcerers, and the second part is the second part that is the identification of the God whom they now recognize as the Lord of Moses and Aaron. The identification of the God as the Lord of Moses and Aaron was, according to some of the commentators, a means of making it clear that the sorcerers were prostrating to the One God, and not to Pharaoh, who also considered himself a god (28:38). The logic of the identification is the logic that is worth stating: the sorcerers who identify the God as the Lord of Moses and Aaron are the sorcerers who are in the ones who distinguish the lordship of God from the lordship of Pharaoh, and the sorcerers who distinguish the lordship of God from the lordship of Pharaoh are the sorcerers who are in the ones who confess the belief in the One God the confrontation of the two lordships, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 104. The commentary of al-Rāzī also suggests that the sorcerers invoked both Moses and Aaron here, because if they had only mentioned "the Lord of Moses," Pharaoh might still think that they were prostrating to him, since Pharaoh had "raised" Moses as a child, on the root of the word for "Lord." The logic of the suggestion is the logic that is worth stating: the sorcerers who mention the Lord of Moses and Aaron are the sorcerers who are in the ones who close the possibility of the confusion between the lordship of God and the lordship of Pharaoh, and the sorcerers who close the possibility of the confusion are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the objects of the wisdom of the speech. Aaron, the brother of Moses, is also identified as the prophet in the Qur'an (19:53). As in the Biblical account, he is sent by God to accompany Moses to Egypt, at the request of Moses, to confront Pharaoh (10:75; 28:34–35), and he also served as the close aide of Moses as he wandered through the desert with the Israelites (7:142; 20:25–36). The verse, in its form, is the form of the second part of the confession of the sorcerers, and the second part of the confession is the part that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the reaction of Pharaoh in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:123

> **Pharaoh said, “You believe in him before I grant you leave! This is surely a plot you have devised in the city, that you might expel its people therefrom. Soon you shall know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Outrage of Pharaoh**

The verse states the reaction of Pharaoh to the confession of the sorcerers, and the reaction is the reaction that is the outrage: *You believe in him before I grant you leave!* Pharaoh is outraged that the sorcerers would believe in Moses before Pharaoh "grants them leave." In contrast to the sorcerers, who immediately recognized that the power of Moses came from the One God, Pharaoh considered the feat of Moses no more than the trick of a sorcerer, albeit a superior one. The logic of the contrast is the logic that is worth stating in full: the sorcerers who recognize the power of God and Pharaoh who considers the feat of Moses the trick of a sorcerer are the two men who are in the two states of the recognition of the truth, and the two states of the recognition of the truth are the two states that are the state of the one who recognizes the truth and the state of the one who does not recognize the truth. The sorcerer who knows the art of the sorcery is the sorcerer who recognizes the truth when the truth is displayed before him, and the king who is in the arrogance is the king who does not recognize the truth when the truth is displayed before him. The two men are the two forms of the same teaching: the truth is displayed before the two men, and the two men are the two responses to the truth, and the two responses to the truth are the two responses that are the belief and the disbelief.

**The Accusation of the Plot**

The verse states the accusation of Pharaoh, and the accusation is the accusation that is the description of the competition as the plot: *This is surely a plot you have devised in the city, that you might expel its people therefrom*. Pharaoh describes the entire competition, including the victory of Moses and the submission of the sorcerers, as the plot devised by Moses and the sorcerers together to expel the people of Egypt from Egypt, so that the Israelites could settle there. According to one report, before the competition began, Moses approached the head of the sorcerers to ask if they would believe in him and witness to the truth of his message if Moses were victorious, and the sorcerers agreed that they would. Pharaoh reportedly overheard the exchange, and when it came to pass, he declared the whole affair to have been the prearranged plot. The logic of the accusation is the logic that is worth stating: the king who accuses the prophet of the plot is the king who is in the one who cannot accept the truth, and the king who cannot accept the truth is the king who is in the one who accuses the prophet of the plot, on the form of the teaching of the accusation of the sorcery in the commentary of verse 109. The warning of Pharaoh, "soon you shall know," is the warning that is the statement that the sorcerers will know the terrible punishment that Pharaoh intends to impose on them, on the form of the statement of the next verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the reaction of Pharaoh, and the statement of the reaction of Pharaoh is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the threat of Pharaoh in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:124

> **I shall surely cut off your hands and your feet on alternate sides; then I shall surely crucify you all!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Threat of the Cruel Punishment**

The verse states the threat of Pharaoh to the sorcerers, and the threat is the threat that is the cutting off of the hands and the feet on the alternate sides, and the crucifixion: *I shall surely cut off your hands and your feet on alternate sides; then I shall surely crucify you all*. The punishment of the verse is similar to the punishment ordained for those who wage war against God and His Messenger and endeavor to work corruption upon the earth (5:33), and the similarity of the two punishments is the similarity that is the gravity of the act of the sorcerers who turn against Pharaoh, and the gravity of the act of the sorcerers is the gravity that is the act that is against the king the act of the war against the king.

The commentators report that the first person to execute the punishment of the cutting off of the hands and the feet on the alternate sides and the crucifixion was Pharaoh, and Pharaoh crucified his victims on the palm trees on the banks of the Nile. The detail of the crucifixion on the palm trees of the banks of the Nile is the detail that is mentioned in the threat of Pharaoh as uttered in the other sūrah (20:71). The logic of the threat is the logic that is worth stating: the king who threatens the believers with the cruel punishment is the king who is in the one who uses the fear of the death the means of the preservation of the obedience of the people, and the king who uses the fear of the death in the form of the means of the preservation of the obedience of the people is the king who is in the one who is the object of the wisdom of God in the next verse. The threat of Pharaoh is the threat that is the contrast of the teaching: the sorcerers who believe in God in the face of the threat of the cruel punishment are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the examples of the strength of the faith in the face of the fear of the death. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the threat of Pharaoh, and the statement of the threat of Pharaoh is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the response of the sorcerers in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:125

> **They said, “Truly we turn unto our Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turning unto the Lord**

The verse states the response of the sorcerers to the threat of Pharaoh, and the response is the response that is the turning unto the Lord: *Truly we turn unto our Lord*. The statement of the turning unto the Lord of the sorcerers is the statement that indicates that they were not troubled by the threat of the death, since they now looked only toward the meeting with God with love and longing, and with the hope that they would be rewarded for the pain they suffered under the torments of Pharaoh. The logic of the response is the logic that is worth stating in full: the sorcerers who turn unto their Lord in the face of the threat of the death are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are not troubled by the threat of the death, and the sorcerers who are not troubled by the threat of the death are the sorcerers who are in the ones who look only toward the meeting with God, and the sorcerers who look only toward the meeting with God are the sorcerers who are in the ones who are the objects of the reward of God for the pain they suffer. The man who is not troubled by the threat of the death is the man who is in the one who is the object of the strength of the faith, and the man who is the object of the strength of the faith is the man who is in the one who turns unto his Lord in the face of the threat of the death. The verse, in its form, is the form of the first part of the response of the sorcerers, and the first part of the response is the part that is in the form of the introduction of the second part of the response in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:126

> **You take vengeance upon us only because we believed in the signs of our Lord when they came unto us. Our Lord, shower us with patience, and let us die as submitters.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complaint and the Supplication**

The verse is the second part of the response of the sorcerers, and the second part is the second part that is the two acts: the complaint to Pharaoh, and the supplication to God. The complaint of the sorcerers to Pharaoh is the complaint that is the statement of the cause of the vengeance: *You take vengeance upon us only because we believed in the signs of our Lord when they came unto us*. The "signs of our Lord" of the verse refers to the feats performed by Moses, and the feats of Moses are the feats that are the signs of God that the sorcerers believe in. The humble complaint of the sorcerers is similar to the words of the secret believer in the court of Pharaoh, who asks Pharaoh if he would kill a man for saying, "My Lord is God" (40:28), and is also on the form of the other verses that chastise those who would be vengeful toward others simply on account of their belief in God and His signs (5:59; 85:9).

The supplication of the sorcerers is the supplication that is the two requests: the request of the patience, and the request of the death as the submitters: *Our Lord, shower us with patience, and let us die as submitters*. The request of the patience is the request that is on the form of the request of the other verse (2:250), in which the Israelites facing the army make the same supplication, and the request of the patience is the request that God aid them in remaining steadfast in the face of the torture and the death at the hands of Pharaoh, lest they revert to the disbelief under this extreme duress. According to some of the commentators, the painful death suffered by the sorcerers purified them of their past sins. The word for "submitters" is the same word that is used in the universal sense of those who submit themselves fully to the One God. Pharaoh reportedly had the sorcerers killed and crucified that very day, and several of the early commentators observed that the sorcerers "began the day as sorcerers and ended it as martyrs." The request of the "let us die as submitters" can also be translated "take us to Thyself as submitters," and might therefore be understood as the plea for God to take them to Himself immediately so that they might be spared the punishment of Pharaoh. The two similar accounts of the response of the sorcerers to the threats of Pharaoh are in the other sūrahs (20:72–73; 26:50–51).

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:127

> **The notables among Pharaoh’s people said, “Will you leave Moses and his people to work corruption in the land and to leave you and your gods?” He said, “We shall slay their sons and spare their women. Truly we are above them, dominant.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Concern of the Notables**

The verse states the act of the notables of the people of Pharaoh, and the act is the act that is the concern: *Will you leave Moses and his people to work corruption in the land and to leave you and your gods?* Although Pharaoh killed the sorcerers, he allowed Moses to go free. The notables express the concern that Pharaoh would leave Moses and his people, the Israelites, to live and to worship as they prefer, and to leave Pharaoh and his gods, that is, to abandon the worship of Pharaoh and his gods for the obedience to Moses and the worship of the One God. The commentators are unclear on the identity of the gods of Pharaoh mentioned in the verse: some say he worshipped the sun or certain sacred cattle, hence the inclination of the Israelites to make the idol in the form of the calf in verse 148. Still others assert that Pharaoh was himself worshipped, but did not worship anything himself, or that the gods referred to here were the idols that Pharaoh had made and that he had commanded the people to worship as a way of worshipping him. The question of the notables might also be rendered, "Will you leave Moses and his people to work corruption upon the earth, when they have left you and your gods?" The logic of the concern of the notables is the logic that is on the form of the teaching of the previous verses: the notables who are concerned about the leaving of Moses and his people are the notables who are in the ones who want to preserve the worship of Pharaoh and his gods, and the notables who want to preserve the worship of Pharaoh and his gods are the notables who are in the ones who are the objects of the threat of Pharaoh in the second part of the verse.

**The Threat of the Slaying of the Sons**

The verse states the reply of Pharaoh to the concern of the notables, and the reply is the reply that is the threat: *We shall slay their sons and spare their women. Truly we are above them, dominant*. The threat of the slaying of the sons and the sparing of the women is consistent with the means of the collective punishment of Pharaoh mentioned elsewhere (7:141; 2:49; 14:6; 28:4; 40:25). Although Pharaoh did not harm Moses, out of fear of his great power, he reportedly carried out his threat upon his Israelite followers, hoping to weaken Moses indirectly by terrifying his supporters. The statement of the "we are above them, dominant" of the verse is the statement that is worth stating: the word for "dominant" is the same word that is a Name or an Attribute of God in the Qur'an (6:18: He is Dominant over His servants), and the statement of Pharaoh of himself in the terms used elsewhere in the Qur'an for God alone suggests the claim of Pharaoh of the divinity and the lordship for himself, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (26:29; 28:38; 79:24). The logic of the claim of Pharaoh is the logic that is worth stating: the king who speaks of himself in the terms of God is the king who is in the one who claims the divinity for himself, and the king who claims the divinity for himself is the king who is in the one who is the object of the destruction of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the concern of the notables and the threat of Pharaoh, and the statement of the concern of the notables and the threat of Pharaoh are the statement of the concern of the notables and the threat of Pharaoh that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the advice of Moses in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:128

> **Moses said unto his people, “Seek help from God and be patient. Truly the land belongs to God; He bequeaths it to whomsoever He will among His servants. And the end belongs to the reverent.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Advice of the Seeking of Help and the Patience**

The verse states the advice of Moses to his people when they are facing the terrible punishment of Pharaoh described in verse 127, and the advice is the advice that is the two commands: *Seek help from God and be patient*. The command of the seeking of help from God is the command that is on the form of the command of the opening sūrah (1:5), and the command of the patience is the command that is on the form of the command of the other verses (2:45; 153), in which the believers are enjoined to seek help in the patience and the prayer as the general spiritual attitude, for God is with the patient (8:46). The logic of the advice is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who advises his people to seek help from God and to be patient is the prophet who is in the one who teaches the people the attitude of the faith in the state of the oppression, and the attitude of the faith in the state of the oppression is the attitude that is the seeking of the aid of God the seeking of the aid of the prayer and the patience, and the seeking of the aid of the prayer and the patience is the seeking that is the strength of the faith in the state of the oppression.

**The Land of God and the End of the Reverent**

The verse continues with the reminder of the ownership of the land, and the reminder is the reminder that is the statement of the belonging of the land to God: *Truly the land belongs to God; He bequeaths it to whomsoever He will among His servants*. The "land" of the verse means the domain of Pharaoh, and the bequeathing of the land by God to whomever He wills among His servants is the bequeathing that is fulfilled in verse 137 of the sūrah, in which God bequeaths the eastern and the western parts of the land that He blessed to the Israelites, as the reward for their patience. The reminder of the ownership of the land by God is the reminder that is the teaching that the sovereignty of the king is the sovereignty that is the loan of God, and the loan of God is the loan that is the thing that is returned to God and bequeathed by God to whomever He wills. The verse closes with the promise that is the general promise also made in the other verses (11:49; 28:83): *And the end belongs to the reverent*. The "end" of the verse may refer to the reward of the reverent in the Hereafter, to the later worldly success of the Israelites in vanquishing their enemies and settling in the land, or to both. The logic of the promise is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are the objects of the promise of the end are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the patience, and the people who are the objects of the patience are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the end that belongs to the reverent. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the advice of Moses, and the statement of the advice of Moses is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the response of the Israelites in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:129

> **They said, “We were persecuted before you came to us, and after you came to us.” He said, “It may be that your Lord will destroy your enemies and make you vicegerents upon the earth, that He may observe how you behave.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complaint of the Israelites**

The verse states the response of the Israelites to the advice of Moses, and the response is the response that is the complaint: *We were persecuted before you came to us, and after you came to us*. The complaint of the Israelites is the complaint that is the statement of the persecution before the coming of Moses, since the Qur'an elsewhere indicates that the punishment of the slaying of the sons was inflicted by Pharaoh upon the Israelites at the time of the infancy of Moses (28:4–9), and the persecution after the coming of Moses, in the form of the threat of verse 127. The display of the power of Moses had raised the hopes of the Israelites of being delivered from the oppression of Pharaoh, but when they heard Pharaoh announce the same brutal collective punishment that he had inflicted on them earlier, they became despondent. The logic of the complaint is the logic that is worth stating: the people who complain of the continuation of the persecution are the people who are in the ones who are in the state of the despondency, and the people who are in the state of the despondency are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the strengthening of the resolve by the prophet, on the form of the teaching of the reply of Moses in the second part of the verse.

**The Vicegerents Upon the Earth**

The verse states the reply of Moses to the complaint of the Israelites, and the reply is the reply that is the strengthening of the resolve: *It may be that your Lord will destroy your enemies and make you vicegerents upon the earth, that He may observe how you behave*. The word for "vicegerents" is the same word that the sūrah used in verse 69 and verse 74, and the successive vicegerency of the different peoples is the recurring idea of this sūrah, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (6:165). The logic of the reply is the logic that is worth stating in full: the prophet who promises the people the destruction of the enemies and the vicegerency upon the earth is the prophet who is in the one who strengthens the resolve of the people by the promise of the victory, and the promise of the victory of the prophet is the promise that is the statement of the purpose of the victory: *that He may observe how you behave*. The purpose of the victory is the purpose that is the observation of the behavior of the people, and the observation of the behavior of the people is the observation that is the teaching that the victory is the victory that is the test of the people, and the test of the people is the test that is the continuation of the worship of the One God and the obedience to His commands. The man who is the object of the victory is the man who is in the one who is the object of the test of the victory, and the man who is the object of the test of the victory is the man who is in the one who is the object of the observation of God. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the reply of Moses, and the statement of the reply of Moses is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the affliction of the House of Pharaoh in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:130

> **And We indeed afflicted the House of Pharaoh with drought and a shortage of crops, that haply they would be reminded.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Drought and the Shortage of the Crops**

The verse states the act of God upon the House of Pharaoh, and the act is the act that is the affliction with the drought and the shortage of the crops: *And We indeed afflicted the House of Pharaoh with drought and a shortage of crops*. The verse is on the form of the similar verse (133), in which the other plagues sent against Pharaoh and his people are mentioned. The affliction of the people of Pharaoh with the drought and the shortage of the crops during the time of the mission of Moses is consistent with the other verses that indicate that the adversity suffered by a people in connection with the coming of a prophet is meant to make them humble and to cause them to hearken to the message of God, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (6:42) and the teaching of verse 94 of the sūrah. The phrase of the "that haply they would be reminded" of the verse is the phrase that is the statement of the purpose of the affliction: the famine and the drought were meant to be the admonition to Pharaoh and his people and to frighten them, so that they would repent and believe in God.

**The Softening of the Heart and the Hardness of Pharaoh**

The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full. The adversity in general may also have the effect of the "softening of the heart," and the softening of the heart of the adversity is the softening that is not in the case of Pharaoh, whose heart, according to the Biblical account, was increasingly hardened by the successive plagues, and here too he remains the unrepentant. The commentary of some of the commentators sees this verse as the proof that God does not compel the people toward the belief or the disbelief, but rather arranges the matters so as to encourage the belief, but then allows the people to choose how they will react. The other commentators cast doubt on this interpretation, noting that these apparent trials only further entrenched Pharaoh in the disbelief. The two readings of the commentators are the two readings that are the teaching that the affliction of God is the affliction that is the means of the encouragement of the belief, and that the choice of the people is the choice that is the response of the people to the means of the encouragement of the belief. The man who is the object of the affliction is the man who is in the one who is given the means of the reminder, and the man who is given the means of the reminder is the man who is in the one who chooses the response to the means of the reminder. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the affliction of the House of Pharaoh, and the statement of the affliction is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the response of Pharaoh in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:131

> **But whenever good came to them, they would say, “This is ours.” And if an evil befell them, they would consider it an ill omen on account of Moses and those who were with him. Nay, their ill omen lies with God, though most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Error of the Attribution**

The verse states the response of Pharaoh and his supporters to the good and the evil, and the response is the response that is the two parts: the response to the good, and the response to the evil. The first part is the response to the good, and the response to the good is the response that is the claim of the ownership: *But whenever good came to them, they would say, "This is ours."* Pharaoh and his supporters would consider any good fortune they enjoyed to be something they deserved, rather than a blessing for which they should be grateful. The claim of the ownership of the good is the claim that is the removal of the gratitude from the state of the good, and the removal of the gratitude from the state of the good is the removal that is the attribution of the good to the self rather than the attribution of the good to God.

**The Ill Omen of Moses**

The second part of the verse is the response to the evil, and the response to the evil is the response that is the attribution of the evil to Moses: *And if an evil befell them, they would consider it an ill omen on account of Moses and those who were with him*. The "ill omen" of the verse is the word that the Thamūd used for Ṣāli in the other sūrah (27:47), and the attribution of the evil to the prophet is the attribution that is on the form of the teaching of the other verses (4:78–79), in which certain of the Jews or the hypocrites in Madinah considered any good that befell the city to be from God, but any evil to be on account of the presence of the Prophet ﷺ. The response of the Qur'an in the other verses is the response that all the good is from God, and all the evil is from oneself, and the response of the Qur'an in the present verse is the response that is the statement that the ill omen of the people is the ill omen that lies with God, meaning that all the good or the bad fortune happens according to the Will and the Wisdom of God. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who attribute the good to themselves and the evil to the prophet are the people who are in the ones who make the error of the attribution, and the people who make the error of the attribution are the people who are in the ones who are in the removal of the acknowledgment of the wisdom of God from the state of the good and the evil. The man who attributes the good to himself is the man who is in the one who removes the gratitude, and the man who attributes the evil to the prophet is the man who is in the one who removes the trust in the wisdom of God. The two attributions of the man are the two attributions that are the two errors of the state of the man who is the disbelief. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the response of Pharaoh and his supporters, and the statement of the response is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the refusal of the signs in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:132

> **And they said, “Whatever sign you may bring to bewitch us thereby, we will not believe in you.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal in Advance**

The verse states the declaration of Pharaoh and his people, and the declaration is the declaration that is the refusal in advance of the signs: *Whatever sign you may bring to bewitch us thereby, we will not believe in you*. The verse encapsulates one of the key ideas presented in this account as a whole, namely, that no miraculous sign, no matter how powerful or convincing, can change the hearts of those who stubbornly refuse to believe. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who refuse the signs in advance are the people who are in the ones who pre-decide the disbelief, and the people who pre-decide the disbelief are the people who are in the ones who render the signs useless. The sign of God is the sign that is the thing that serves the one who is inwardly disposed to the belief, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 111, and the sign of God is the sign that is the thing that cannot serve the one who is in the hardness of the heart, and the one who is in the hardness of the heart is the one who is Pharaoh and his notables. In verse 146 of the sūrah, the stubbornness of the people is engendered by the pride, since God "turns away from His signs" those who, like Pharaoh and his notables, wax arrogant upon the earth. The idea that some are impervious to all the signs of God is the idea that is found in the other verses (6:25; 10:96–97). The man who refuses the signs in advance is the man who is in the one who is the object of the turning away of God from his heart, and the man who is the object of the turning away of God from his heart is the man who is in the one who is the object of the destruction of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the refusal of the signs, and the statement of the refusal of the signs is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the sending of the punishments in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:133

> **So We sent against them the flood and the locusts, and the lice and the frogs and the blood—signs expounded. But they waxed arrogant, and they were a guilty people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Five Punishments**

The verse lists the five punishments that God sent against Pharaoh and his people in response to their failure to believe in the One God and in the prophethood of Moses, and to their continued oppression of the Israelites. The five punishments are the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, and the blood, and four of the five are identical to four of the ten plagues visited upon the people of Pharaoh in the Biblical account (Exodus 9–11). In the other sūrah (17:101), the Qur'an states that Moses was given the nine clear signs, although these nine are understood as including the miracles of his staff and his hand, not just the punishments visited upon Pharaoh. The tradition gives the details of the five punishments, and the details are the details that are worth stating. The flood is the flood that is translated by the word that is related to the word "typhoon," and is the word also used to describe the flood of Noah (29:14), which usually connotes the complete deluge that brings the sudden and the rapid death. Some of the commentators, however, interpret the word to mean the death that comes from all sides or by all the means, since it derives from the root meaning "to surround," and the view is supported by the report in which the Prophet ﷺ glossed the word simply as "death." According to one report, the rain persisted for eight days and flooded the houses of the Egyptians, but did not enter those of the Israelites. The locusts consumed most of the crops in the fields, and when this did not cause Pharaoh to repent, the lice, which may denote a variety of tiny insects, including the fleas or the ticks, consumed the rest of the crops. The continued stubbornness of Pharaoh led to the subsequent punishments in the form of the frogs, which filled the houses of the Egyptians and ruined them, and the blood, which was what any water drawn by the Egyptians turned into, while the water drawn from the same sources by the Israelites was clean.

**The Signs Expounded and the Arrogance**

The verse describes the punishments as the "signs expounded," and the phrase is interpreted by some of the commentators to mean the signs that any reasonable person would know came from God, and is interpreted by the other commentators to mean the successive signs, that is, distinct from one another in time. According to one report, each punishment lasted a week, and when it was lifted on account of the plea of Pharaoh and the supplication of Moses to God, the state of the repentance of Pharaoh lasted only a month, after which he resumed his disbelieving and oppressive ways. The statement of the "they waxed arrogant, and they were a guilty people" of the verse is the statement that is the teaching that the people who waxed arrogant in the face of the signs are the people who are truly the guilty people, stubborn and incorrigible in their sinful behavior. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who waxed arrogant in the face of the five signs are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the sixth punishment in the next verse, and the people who are the objects of the sixth punishment are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the destruction of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the five punishments, and the statement of the five punishments is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the reaction of Pharaoh in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:134

> **Then when the torment came down upon them, they said, “O Moses! Call upon your Lord for us by the covenant He has made with you. If you lift this torment from us, we shall surely believe in you, and we shall surely send forth the Children of Israel with you.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Torment and the Imploring**

The verse states the reaction of Pharaoh and his people to the coming down of the torment upon them, and the reaction is the reaction that is the imploring of Moses: *O Moses! Call upon your Lord for us by the covenant He has made with you*. The word for "torment" is the word that can mean the filth and the defilement as well as the divine punishment, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 71 and the other verse (29:34). According to some of the commentators, the torment of the verse refers to the sudden illness that took the lives of seventy thousand Egyptians in a single night, while sparing the Israelites who had marked their doors with the blood of the sheep, and the details suggest that this refers to the same event as the plague that, according to the Bible, struck the firstborn among the Egyptians (Exodus 11–12). The other commentators note that the torment may be the collective reference to the various punishments inflicted upon the people of Pharaoh in verse 133, and that the present verses describe the reaction of Pharaoh and his associates to each one. The phrase of the "call upon your Lord for us by the covenant He has made with you" of the verse is the phrase that refers to the special relationship that they understand Moses to have had with God, given his ability to perform the miraculous signs.

**The Promise of the Belief**

The verse states the promise of Pharaoh and his people, and the promise is the promise that is the two statements: *If you lift this torment from us, we shall surely believe in you, and we shall surely send forth the Children of Israel with you*. The promise of the belief and the promise of the release of the Children of Israel are the two promises that are the human tendency to call upon God in the moments of the peril and to make the promises of the worship and the belief that are soon broken when the peril has passed, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (6:63–64; 10:22–23; 29:65; 30:33; 31:32). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who make the promises in the state of the peril are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the test of the state of the peril, and the people who are the objects of the test of the state of the peril are the people who are in the ones who are the examples of the teaching that the promise of the state of the peril is the promise that is the promise that is broken by the state of the ease. The man who makes the promise in the state of the peril is the man who is in the one who is the object of the next verse, in which the verse states that they reneged. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the imploring and the promise, and the statement of the imploring and the promise is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the lifting of the torment in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:135

> **But when We lifted the torment from them, for a term they were to fulfill, behold, they reneged.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lifting of the Torment and the Term**

The verse states the act of God after the promise of Pharaoh and his people, and the act is the act that is the lifting of the torment: *But when We lifted the torment from them, for a term they were to fulfill*. The lifting of the torment of God is the lifting that is the response of God to the imploring of the people, and the lifting of the torment is the lifting that is the lifting for the term that the people were to fulfill. The "term" of the verse is said by some of the commentators to refer to the period of the respite before the people would be destroyed completely. The logic of the term is the logic that is worth stating: God who lifts the torment for the term is God who is the one who gives the people the time of the fulfillment of the promise, and the time of the fulfillment of the promise is the time that is the test of the people, and the test of the people is the test that is the keeping of the promise or the breaking of the promise.

**The Reneging of the People**

The verse states the act of the people after the lifting of the torment, and the act is the act that is the reneging: *behold, they reneged*. The reneging of the people refers to their obstinate refusal to recognize the prophethood of Moses or to release the Israelites, as they had promised in verse 134. The logic of the reneging is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who renege on the promise after the lifting of the torment are the people who are in the ones who confirm the teaching of the promise of the state of the peril, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the previous verse. The people who renege on the promise are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the vengeance of God in the next verse, and the people who are the objects of the vengeance of God are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the destruction of the sūrah. The man who reneges on the promise is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the promise of the state of the peril is the promise that is the promise of the state of the peril, and that the promise of the state of the ease is the promise that is the promise of the keeping of the state of the ease. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the lifting of the torment and the reneging of the people, and the statement of the lifting of the torment and the reneging of the people are the statement of the lifting of the torment and the reneging of the people that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the vengeance of God in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:136

> **So We took vengeance upon them and drowned them in the sea for their having denied Our signs and for having been heedless of them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Vengeance and the Drowning**

The verse states the final act of the story of Pharaoh, and the act is the act that is the vengeance and the drowning: *So We took vengeance upon them and drowned them in the sea for their having denied Our signs and for having been heedless of them*. The taking of the vengeance of God means that He punished them for their evil deeds and because they were heedless of the signs and the clear proofs of the prophethood of Moses. The Qur'an, like the Bible, recounts that Pharaoh and his hosts were drowned in the sea, which Moses had miraculously parted to allow the Israelites to pass through unharmed. When Pharaoh and his men attempted to pursue them through the parted waters, the sea returned to its normal state and drowned them, on the form of the statement of the other verses (2:50; 8:54; 10:90; 17:103; 26:65–66; 28:40; 43:55; 44:24; 51:40).

**The Word of the Sea**

The word for "sea" of the verse is the word that is similar to the Hebrew word used in the Biblical reference to the sea that Moses parted (Exodus 14–15), and the word, rather than the more common Arabic word for sea, is used in the Qur'an only in connection with the Mosaic narratives (20:39, 78, 97; 28:7, 40; 51:40). The word may also refer to the sea of the unfathomable depths, or, alternately, to the loud roar of the sea or to its vastness. The particularity of the word of the sea of the Mosaic narratives is the particularity that is the teaching that the sea of the Mosaic narratives is the sea that is the sea of the unfathomable depths, and the sea of the unfathomable depths is the sea that is the sea of the power of God, and the sea of the power of God is the sea that is the sea of the destruction of the people of Pharaoh. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are drowned in the sea of the unfathomable depths are the people who are in the ones who are the objects of the power of God the sea, and the people who are the objects of the power of God in the form of the sea are the people who are in the ones who are the examples of the teaching that the power of God is the power that is the power that drowns the people who deny the signs of God and who are heedless of them. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the vengeance and the drowning, and the statement of the vengeance and the drowning is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the bequeathing of the land in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:137

> **And We bequeathed unto the people who were oppressed the eastern and western parts of the land that We blessed. And the most beautiful Word of thy Lord was fulfilled for the Children of Israel because they were patient. And We demolished all that Pharaoh and his people had wrought and that which they used to build.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inheritance Given to the Oppressed**

The verse closes the Exodus narrative in this sūrah by naming who ends up holding the land. *Mustaḍʿafūn* — "those who were oppressed" — comes from the root ḍ-ʿ-f, "weakness." The form is not passive but reflexive-intensive: it describes people who were *held* in weakness, deliberately kept weak by someone stronger. Pharaoh's system did not merely happen to disadvantage the Israelites; it was engineered to keep them feeble, and the Qur'an elsewhere records his method in blunt terms: he "slew their sons and spared their women" (28:4). Weakness imposed from outside is what the word carries.

That this same word describes the inheritors is the theological point of the verse. The land is not awarded to the militarily strongest, the most numerous, or the most politically accomplished. It is bequeathed to the people who were kept weak and who nevertheless held. God's bequest runs in the opposite direction from conventional power, and the verse says so by choosing the very term the oppressor used to define his victims.

**Patience as the Condition of the Promise**

> *And the most beautiful Word of thy Lord was fulfilled for the Children of Israel because they were patient.*

The causal particle matters. Fulfilment came *because they were patient* (ṣabarū). Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as the direct realisation of what Moses told his people in verse 128 — "Seek help in God and be patient" — so the sūrah answers its own earlier command within nine verses. The promise was not a guarantee that patience would be unnecessary; patience was the mechanism by which the promise operated.

This is worth stating plainly, because it is easily misheard. Ṣabr is not passivity. It is the refusal to abandon a position under pressure, which is an active discipline sustained over time. The Israelites did nothing to part the sea. What they did was continue to hold onto God's word through years of infanticide, forced labour, and ridicule — the period when holding on looked like nothing at all. The verse credits exactly that.

Read generally, as many commentators do, the principle extends beyond one historical case: whoever meets adversity with patience and the expectation of Divine succour will be granted deliverance. The historical instance is the proof-of-concept, not the limit of the rule.

**Which Land Was Blessed**

Commentators differ on the referent of *the land that We blessed*, and the disagreement is instructive rather than merely geographical. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabarsī take it to be Syria and Palestine (Shām), because that is the region the Qur'an elsewhere ordains for the Israelites: "O my people! Enter the holy land which God has ordained for you" (5:21). Al-Rāzī records others who take it to be Pharaoh's own domain in Egypt, and a third position holds that both are intended.

The ground for calling it blessed is fertility and abundance — the land is blessed in the concrete sense of producing. But the Qur'an is careful not to make the blessing an exclusive ethnic property. Of the same region it says elsewhere that it is holy and blessed for all peoples (21:71). Inheritance here is tied to a moral condition, not to descent, which is why the verse attaches it to patience rather than to lineage.

The phrase *the eastern and western parts of the land* is best read as an idiom for the whole of it — east and west together meaning every direction, the complete territory rather than a partition of it.

**What Was Demolished**

> *And We demolished all that Pharaoh and his people had wrought and that which they used to build.*

Al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī and al-Zamakhsharī take this as the destruction of palaces, gardens, fields and orchards. The verse distinguishes two things: what they had *wrought* (yaṣnaʿūn) and what they used to *build* (yaʿrishūn). The pairing covers both the products of their craft and the structures they raised — manufacture and architecture, the movable and the fixed.

The theological weight falls on the futility. Pharaoh's entire claim to authority rested on visible, durable achievement; the Qur'an quotes his boast elsewhere, "Do I not possess the dominion of Egypt and these rivers flowing beneath me?" (43:51). The verse answers by recording that all of it was demolished. Monumental building is precisely the kind of thing a regime uses to argue for its own permanence, and the demolition is the argument against that claim.

The practical lesson is not that building is wrong but that building cannot bear the weight people place on it. Anything made the proof of one's own indispensability is being asked to do work it cannot do, and the verse records what happens when the test is applied.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:138

> **And We brought the Children of Israel across the sea, and they came upon a people clinging to their idols. They said, “O Moses! Make for us a god as they have gods.” He said, “Truly you are an ignorant people!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crossing of the Sea and the People of the Idols**

The verse states the act of God after the drowning of the people of Pharaoh, and the act is the act that is the bringing of the Children of Israel across the sea: *And We brought the Children of Israel across the sea*. The Children of Israel were brought across the sea when Moses miraculously parted it with his staff, by the divine command (2:50; 20:77; 26:63). Shortly thereafter, according to some of the commentators, even before their feet were dry from their walk across the divided sea, they came upon a people clinging to their idols, and the commentators speculate that these may have been the Canaanites or the Lakhmites who had settled in the area, or the descendants of the idolatrous people of Abraham. The idols of the people were said to be in the form of the calf, which is also the form of the idol later made by the Israelites in verse 148 of the sūrah.

**The Demand of the God**

The verse states the demand of the Children of Israel to Moses, and the demand is the demand that is the seeking of the god: *O Moses! Make for us a god as they have gods*. The logic of the demand is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who demand the god from the prophet are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the test of the state of the victory, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 129, and the people who are the objects of the test of the state of the victory are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who fail the test of the state of the victory by the demand of the god. The man who demands the god from the prophet is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the imitation of the people of the idols, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the imitation of the people of the idols is the man who is in the one who is the object of the reproof of the prophet in the second part of the verse. The reply of Moses to the demand of the people is the reply that is the derision of the people as the ignorant people, and the word for "ignorant" is derived from the same root as the word for the pre-Islamic pagan era in Arabia. In both cases, the ignorance refers to the ignorance of the Greatness of God, of the duty to worship Him, and of the prohibition against worshipping anything else. The logic of the reproof is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who reproofs the people as the ignorant people is the prophet who is in the one who teaches the people that the ignorance is the ignorance that is the ignorance of the Greatness of God, and the ignorance of the Greatness of God is the ignorance that is the ignorance that is the root of the error of the worship. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the crossing of the sea and the demand of the god, and the statement of the crossing of the sea and the demand of the god are the statement of the crossing of the sea and the demand of the god that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the reply of Moses in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:139

> **As for these, what they practice shall perish, and vain is that which they used to do.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Perishing of the Practice of the Idolaters**

The verse is the continuation of the reply of Moses, and the continuation is the continuation that is the statement of the perishing of the practice of the idolaters: *As for these, what they practice shall perish, and vain is that which they used to do*. The "these" of the verse refers to the idolaters whom the Israelites have come upon, and the statement of the perishing of the practice of the idolaters is the statement that is the teaching that the practice of the idolaters is the practice that is the practice that perishes, and the practice that perishes is the practice that is the practice that is without the permanence. The statement of the "vain" of the verse is the statement that is the teaching that the worship of the idols is the worship that is the worship that neither brings any benefit nor deflects any harm. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who worship the idols are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the perishing of the practice, and the people who are the objects of the perishing of the practice are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the examples of the teaching that the worship is the worship that is the worship that is the permanence or the worship that is the perishing, and the worship that is the permanence is the worship of God, and the worship that is the perishing is the worship of the idols. The desire of the Israelites for the idol of the verse foreshadows the Israelites making and worshipping the calf in verse 148 of the sūrah, and the foreshadowing of the verse is the foreshadowing that is the teaching that the demand of the god of the verse is the demand that is fulfilled the demand of the calf in the next section of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the perishing of the practice of the idolaters, and the statement of the perishing of the practice of the idolaters is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the continuation of the reply of Moses in the next verse.

## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:140

> **He said, “Shall I seek for you a god other than God, when He has favored you above the worlds?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetorical Question of the Prophet**

The verse is the continuation of the reply of Moses to the demand of the people, and the continuation is the continuation that is the rhetorical question: *Shall I seek for you a god other than God, when He has favored you above the worlds?* The rhetorical question of Moses is the question that is the refusal of the demand of the god, and the refusal of the demand of the god is the refusal that is the statement of the preference of the god of God to the god of the people. The logic of the question is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who asks the rhetorical question of the seeking of the god other than God is the prophet who is in the one who demonstrates the absurdity of the demand of the people, and the prophet who demonstrates the absurdity of the demand of the people is the prophet who is in the one who teaches the people that the god of God is the god that is the god that is above the gods of the peoples.

**The Favoring Above the Worlds**

The verse states the reason of the refusal, and the reason is the reason that is the favoring of God: *when He has favored you above the worlds*. The statement of the favoring of the Israelites above the worlds may mean that God favored them above all other people of their time, or that the miraculous signs and the deliverance that He granted them through Moses were not granted to any other people. The logic of the favoring is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are favored above the worlds are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the favoring of God, and the people who are the objects of the favoring of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are bound to the worship of the One God who favored them above the worlds, and the people who are bound to the worship of the One God who favored them above the worlds are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who make the error of the demand of the god other than God. The man who demands the god other than God after the favoring of God above the worlds is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the ingratitude, and the man who makes the error of the ingratitude is the man who is in the one who is the object of the next section of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the rhetorical question of the prophet, and the statement of the rhetorical question is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the reminder of the salvation in the next verse.
