## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:188

> **Say, “I have no power over what benefit or harm may come to me, save as God wills. Had I knowledge of the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no evil would have touched me. I am naught save a warner and a bearer of glad tidings unto a people who believe.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Non-Power of the Prophet**

The verse states the command of God to the Prophet ﷺ, and the command is the command that is the statement of the non-power: *Say, "I have no power over what benefit or harm may come to me, save as God wills."* The statement of the non-power of the Prophet ﷺ over the benefit and the harm to himself or the others is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verses (10:49; 72:21–22), and the statement is the statement that means that the Prophet ﷺ is as utterly subject to the divine will as are all the other human beings. The logic of the statement is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who is the object of the statement of the non-power is the prophet who is in the one who is subject to the divine will, and the prophet who is subject to the divine will is the prophet who is in the one who is the example of the subjectivity of the human beings to the divine will.

**The Knowledge of the Unseen**

The verse states the argument of the Prophet ﷺ, and the argument is the argument that is the hypothetical of the knowledge of the unseen: *Had I knowledge of the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no evil would have touched me*. The "knowledge of the unseen" of the verse includes the knowledge of all the realms of reality that transcend the ordinary ken of the human beings, including the knowledge of the celestial realm, of the inner intentions, thoughts, and spiritual destiny of the others, and of the Hour, and of all the future events. In this verse, the Prophet ﷺ argues that if he had the access to such knowledge, particularly regarding the future, he could have used it to prevent the harm or the evil to himself and acquired much good, meaning the accumulation of the good works, although some of the commentators indicate that, in theory, this could also mean the acquisition of the worldly benefits. According to one report, some of the Quraysh asked the Prophet ﷺ if God had informed him about the time of the rising and the falling prices, that they might benefit from this information in their mercantile activity. The logic of the argument is the logic that is worth stating in full: the prophet who argues that he has no knowledge of the unseen is the prophet who is in the one who demonstrates the non-power of the prophet over the unseen, and the prophet who demonstrates the non-power of the prophet over the unseen is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the knowledge of the unseen is the knowledge that is the knowledge of God alone, and the knowledge of God alone is the knowledge that is the knowledge that is not the object of the access of the prophet, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the previous verse. The argument of the prophet is the argument that is the teaching that the one who claims the knowledge of the unseen is the one who is in the one who is the object of the rebuttal of the verse.

**The Warner and the Bearer of Glad Tidings**

The verse closes with the statement of the Prophet ﷺ about himself, and the statement is the statement that is the two descriptions: *I am naught save a warner and a bearer of glad tidings unto a people who believe*. The description of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and the other prophets as simply the "warners" and the "bearers of glad tidings" is the description that is repeated throughout the Qur'an (2:119, 213; 4:165; 5:19; 6:48; 11:2; 17:105; 18:56; 25:56; 33:45; 34:28; 48:8). The logic of the two descriptions is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who describes himself as the warner and the bearer of glad tidings is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the two functions, and the two functions of the prophet are the two functions that are the warning of the people from the punishment and the bearing of the glad tidings of the mercy, and the warning of the people from the punishment and the bearing of the glad tidings of the mercy are the two functions that are the teaching that the prophet is the prophet who is the warner and the bearer of glad tidings rather than the prophet who is the king or the lord. The man who believes in the prophet who is the warner and the bearer of glad tidings is the man who is in the one who is the object of the glad tidings, and the man who is the object of the glad tidings is the man who is in the one who is the object of the mercy of God, on the form of the statement of the "unto a people who believe" of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the non-power of the prophet and the argument of the knowledge of the unseen and the statement of the two descriptions, and the statement of the non-power of the prophet and the argument of the knowledge of the unseen and the statement of the two descriptions are the statement of the non-power of the prophet and the argument of the knowledge of the unseen and the statement of the two descriptions that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the creation from the single soul in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:189

> **He it is Who created you from a single soul, and made from it its mate, that he might find rest in her. Then, when he covered her, she bore a light burden, and carried it about. But when she had grown heavy, they called upon God, their Lord, “If Thou giveth us a healthy child, we shall surely be among the thankful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From a Single Soul**

The verse traces the whole human race to one created soul, then to the mate made from it, and states the purpose of that making: rest. Only then does it turn to the couple's own story — conception, a burden carried lightly and then heavily, and a petition made under that weight.

The *single soul* (nafs wāḥidah) is commonly identified with Adam and its mate with Eve by al-Jawzī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, as at 4:1; see also 39:6. The commentators note, however, that the terms may describe the generation of human beings from male and female spouses generally — a reading that makes the verse about the structure of the human pair rather than about one ancestral couple alone.

That generality is what allows the verse to move so quickly from creation to a specific married couple's prayer. The account is not a remote origin story. It is presented as the pattern still operating, which is why the sudden shift into the first-person plural of verse 189's second half does not read as an inconsistency.

**Rest as the Purpose of the Pair**

The stated purpose of making the mate is *that he might find rest in her* — sakana, from s-k-n, the root of stillness, settling, dwelling. The same word and the same purpose appear at 30:21: "And among His signs is that He created mates for you from among yourselves, that you might find rest in them, and He established affection and mercy between you."

The commentators draw a substantive conclusion from this. Among the most important benefits of marriage are the emotional and physical comforts each spouse provides the other (see also 2:187), not merely the opportunity for procreation, which 16:72 mentions separately. The two goods are distinguished, and rest is named as the purpose here.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains why rest between spouses is natural in a particular sense: because they were created from a single soul, to find rest in one's spouse is to enjoy love, peace and intimacy together with what is, in origin, not wholly other. He goes further and compares the love for a spouse to the love for a child, insofar as both derive from the sense that the beloved is part of one's own being.

That comparison is doing real work. Love for a child is usually treated as the paradigm case of non-transactional attachment — nobody argues that a parent loves a child because the child is useful. Al-Zamakhsharī places spousal love in the same category and grounds both in the same fact about origin.

**The Light Burden and the Heavy**

> *Then, when he covered her, she bore a light burden, and carried it about. But when she had grown heavy, they called upon God, their Lord.*

The verse describes early pregnancy without naming it. The *light burden* (ḥaml khafīf) is carried about — she goes on moving, working, living — and only later does it become heavy. The shift from light to heavy is the whole of the description, and it is given from inside the experience rather than observed from outside.

What the weight produces is prayer. *They called upon God, their Lord* — the plural is notable, since the carrying has been described in the singular. At the point of need both spouses are in the sentence, and the petition is joint.

The wording of that petition is a conditional promise: *"If Thou giveth us a healthy child, we shall surely be among the thankful."* It is the ordinary bargain of a frightened person, and the verse records it without comment. Verse 190 supplies the comment, and it is severe: when the healthy child is given, they ascribe partners to God with regard to what He gave them. The sūrah sets the sincere-sounding vow beside its outcome and lets the distance between them carry the warning.

**Why the Sequence Is Arranged This Way**

Coming after two hundred verses on prophetic rejection, this passage relocates the problem. The failure it describes is not Pharaoh's open defiance but a private transaction in which gratitude was promised under pressure and withdrawn under relief.

That is a more searching indictment, and it explains why the verse begins with creation and the making of the pair. The goodness on display — rest, companionship, a child — is the very thing that gets misattributed once it arrives. Verse 190's charge of *shirk* is attached to a blessing, not to a hardship, which is the harder case for a reader to recognise in himself.

The verse's practical force lies in that recognition. The petition quoted here is one most people have made in some form, and the sūrah's point is not that making it is wrong but that keeping it is the test. Relief is where gratitude is most easily lost, because the need that produced the promise has passed.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:190

> **Then, when He gave them a healthy child, they ascribed partners unto Him with regard to that which He had given them. Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ascription of the Partners**

The verse states the act of the people when God gave them the healthy child, and the act is the act that is the ascription of the partners: *Then, when He gave them a healthy child, they ascribed partners unto Him with regard to that which He had given them*. Given the tendency of the commentators to gloss the original human soul and its mate, in various Quranic contexts, as the references to Adam and Eve, some of the commentators also interpret the subsequent narrative of the verses as pertaining specifically to Adam and Eve, and in this interpretation, when Adam covered Eve, that is, had the sexual relations with her, Eve bore the light burden, meaning the seed of Adam that she now carried about inside her, and when she had grown heavy with the child, the couple together prayed that the child would be the healthy and the sound. Yet when they were given the sound child, they ascribed the partners unto God, and given this spiritually reprehensible response to the blessing they had received, it is difficult to see how this narrative can be said to concern Adam, who is the prophet according to the Islamic tradition. Therefore, some of the commentators indicate that when the verse turns to the discussion of the ascribing of the partners to God, it is referring not to Adam and Eve, but to their offspring, that is, the human beings in general, yet the verb tenses continue to be in the dual. Given all these considerations, it seems most reasonable to understand this narrative as the parable relating to the married couples in general and demonstrating the human tendency to beseech God when one feels the helpless and the afraid, but to attribute the good fortune to the other natural or supernatural causes after God has answered one's prayers, on the form of the teaching of the similar parables of the other verses (6:63–64; 10:22–23; 17:67; 29:65; 30:33; 31:32).

**The Exaltation of God Above the Partners**

The verse closes with the statement of the exaltation of God, and the statement is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verses (9:31; 10:18; 16:1, 3): *Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe*. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who ascribe the partners to God with regard to that which He has given them are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who make the error of the attribution, and the people who make the error of the attribution are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who attribute the good fortune to the other causes rather than to God, and the people who attribute the good fortune to the other causes rather than to God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the exaltation of God above the partners they ascribe. The logic of the error of the attribution is the logic that is on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 131 of the sūrah, and the logic of the error of the attribution is the logic that is worth stating: the people who attribute the good to themselves or to the other causes are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who make the error of the attribution the ascription of the partners to God, and the people who make the error of the attribution in the form of the ascription of the partners to God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the blessing of God is the blessing that is the blessing that is attributed to God alone, and the blessing that is attributed to God alone is the blessing that is the object of the gratitude of the people rather than the object of the ascription of the partners. The man who ascribes the partners to God with regard to the blessing of God is the man who is in the one who is the object of the exaltation of God, and the man who is the object of the exaltation of God is the man who is in the one who is taught that the exaltation of God is the exaltation that is the exaltation of God above the partners that the people ascribe. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the ascription of the partners and the statement of the exaltation of God, and the statement of the ascription of the partners and the statement of the exaltation of God are the statement of the ascription of the partners and the statement of the exaltation of God that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the rhetorical question of the ascription in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:191

> **Do they ascribe as partners those who created naught and are themselves created?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument of the Creation and the Created**

The verse states the rhetorical question of the argument, and the argument is the argument that is the demonstration of the foolishness of the worship of anything other than God: *Do they ascribe as partners those who created naught and are themselves created?* The rhetorical question of the verse is on the form of the common Quranic argument of the other verses (6:100; 13:16; 16:20; 25:3; 31:11; 35:40; 46:4; 52:35–36), and the argument of the verse is the argument that is the teaching that God is the only Creator, and anything other than Him is itself created. The logic of the argument is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who ascribe the partners to God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who worship the created beings, and the people who worship the created beings are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the argument of the verse, and the people who are the objects of the argument of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the object of the worship is the object that is the creator rather than the object that is the created, and the object of the worship that is the creator is the object that is God alone. The man who worships the created being is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the worship of the created rather than the worship of the creator, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the worship of the created rather than the worship of the creator is the man who is in the one who is the object of the argument of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the first part of the argument of the creation and the created, and the statement of the first part of the argument is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the second part of the argument in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:192

> **Those who can neither help them, nor help themselves?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument of the Helplessness**

The verse is the second part of the argument of the creation and the created, and the second part is the second part that is the rhetorical question of the helplessness: *Those who can neither help them, nor help themselves?* The all the objects of worship other than God are therefore the created beings themselves, and they are ultimately incapable of helping the others or even themselves outside of the will of God, and they are thus unable to bring the benefit or the harm to themselves or the others, on the form of the statement of verse 188 of the sūrah, in which the Prophet ﷺ indicates that he too is incapable in this regard, and the statement is on the form of the statement of the other verses (10:49; 5:76; 6:71; 10:18; 13:16; 21:43; 36:74–75; 20:88–89; 21:66; 22:12; 25:3, 55; 26:71–73), which relate to all the idols and the false deities. The logic of the argument of the helplessness is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who worship the idols are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who worship the objects that cannot help them or themselves, and the people who worship the objects that cannot help them or themselves are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the object of the worship is the object that is the object that is the object of the help rather than the object that is the object that is the object of the helplessness, and the object of the worship that is the object of the help is the object that is God alone, and the object of the worship that is the object of the helplessness is the object that is the idol. The man who worships the idol that cannot help him or itself is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping is the man who is in the one who is the object of the argument of the verse. The two parts of the argument of the verse and the previous verse are the two parts that are the teaching that the object of the worship is the object that is the creator and the helping, and the object of the worship that is the creator and the helping is the object that is God alone. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the second part of the argument of the helplessness, and the statement of the second part of the argument of the helplessness is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the calling to the guidance in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:193

> **And if you call them to guidance, they follow you not. It is the same for you whether you call them or whether you remain silent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Calling to the Guidance**

The verse states the statement of the calling to the guidance, and the statement is the statement that is the two readings: *And if you call them to guidance, they follow you not. It is the same for you whether you call them or whether you remain silent*. The first reading of the statement is the reading that the "them" of the verse refers to the idols themselves, and if one calls the idols to the guidance, they follow one not, that is, they do not respond, and the other reading is the reading that when one calls upon the idols for the guidance, they do not respond to the request, and the idols cannot respond in any way because, as suggested in verse 195 of the sūrah, they do not have the ears with which they hear, and they do not have the faculties of the intellect and the speech. The second reading of the statement is the reading that the "them" of the verse refers to the attempt to call the idolaters, or those who ascribe the partners to God, to the guidance. In either case, because of their inability or unwillingness to respond to this call, it does not matter whether one calls them or whether one remains silent.

**The Imperviousness to the Guidance**

The seeming imperviousness of certain of the people to the guidance and the truth is alluded to metaphorically in verse 179 of the sūrah, in which such people are likened to the cattle, and is also mentioned in the other verses (2:6; 36:10). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the calling to the guidance are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who do not respond to the call, and the people who do not respond to the call are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the calling to the guidance is the calling that is the calling that is the same whether it is made or whether it is not made, and the calling that is the same whether it is made or whether it is not made is the calling that is the teaching that the state of the people is the state that is the imperviousness to the guidance, and the state of the imperviousness to the guidance is the state that is the comparison to the cattle of verse 179 of the sūrah. The man who calls the people to the guidance and who is not responded to is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the calling to the guidance is the duty of the man who calls, and that the response of the people called is the state of the people, and the man who is the object of the teaching is the man who is in the one who is the example of the calling to the guidance in the state of the silence, on the form of the teaching of the command of the turning away from the ignorant in verse 199 of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the calling to the guidance and the statement of the imperviousness to the guidance, and the statement of the calling to the guidance and the statement of the imperviousness to the guidance are the statement of the calling to the guidance and the statement of the imperviousness to the guidance that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the servants like you in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:194

> **Truly those whom they call upon apart from God are servants like you. So call upon them! Let them answer you, if you are truthful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Servants Like You**

The verse opens by reclassifying the objects of worship. They are not rivals to God, nor independent powers, nor intermediaries holding a share of the divine authority. They are *ʿibād* — servants, owned beings — and the qualification that follows is the one that stings: *like you*. *Amthālukum*. The worshipper and the thing worshipped belong to the same category.

The point is not that the idols are evil or false in some dramatic sense but that they are junior. They belong to God and stand under His sovereignty, in the same standing as the people bowing to them. A servant who petitions another servant, over the head of the master who owns them both, has misdescribed the situation he is in.

This is the sūrah's sustained argument against shirk reduced to a single word. Elsewhere the Qur'an disputes the *power* of the false deities; here it disputes their *status*, and the dispute over status is the more damaging of the two, because a being with no power might still be pitied or honoured while a being with the same rank as its worshipper cannot be addressed at all.

**The Challenge to Call**

The verse then does something unusual: it grants the request. *So call upon them.* There is no prohibition issued and no argument offered. The idolaters are told to do exactly what they were doing, and to see what happens.

> *"Let them answer you, if you are truthful."*

The conditional *in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — if you are truthful — is the hinge. Truthfulness here is not sincerity but accuracy: the claim that these beings can hear and respond. The verse proposes an empirical test of a metaphysical claim, and it proposes it in the imperative, which removes the excuse of not having tried.

The challenge is structurally identical to the one the Qur'an puts to those who deny the resurrection — produce your proof, if you are truthful — and it works the same way. A claim that cannot survive being acted on was never a claim about the world. The verse does not need to refute the idolater; it needs him to make one phone call.

**Less Capable Than the Worshipper**

Verse 195 presses the same advantage with a sequence of rhetorical questions that Ibn Kathīr and al-Māwardī treat as the core of the argument. Do the idols have feet to walk on? Hands to grasp with? Eyes to see by? Ears to hear with?

The list is deliberately physical and deliberately descending. Feet, hands, eyes, ears — the equipment of an agent. The idols are not described as weak gods but as insensate objects, and the comparison being forced is not between them and God but between them and the person addressing them.

That is the argument's real edge. How is it that a being with feet and hands and eyes and ears worships a thing with none? The worshipper outranks his god in every capacity the questions name. The verse is not asking the idolater to think more highly of God; it is asking him to notice that he is already, by a wide margin, the more capable of the two.

**Scheme Against Me, and Grant Me No Respite**

The passage closes by turning the challenge from the idols to their worshippers, and the turn is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ himself. He is told to invite the full conspiracy: let them scheme against him together, and let them not give him a moment's respite.

The formula is not new to this sūrah. Noah issues it at 10:71 and Hūd at 11:55, in both cases to a people who have the numbers and the intention to kill him, and in both cases the invitation is genuine rather than rhetorical bravado. At 77:39 the same challenge is put to the deniers on the Day of Judgment itself: *So if you have a scheme, then scheme against Me.*

The reason a prophet can issue it is given elsewhere and does not depend on his own strength. Nothing in the heavens or the earth can thwart God — 35:44 — while God's own scheme holds even where He grants respite, which is the subject of verses 182 and 183 of this sūrah. The respite the disbeliever is enjoying is not evidence that the scheme against him has failed; it is the mechanism by which it is being completed.

Read in sequence the two challenges are one argument. The idols cannot answer when they are called, and their worshippers cannot harm the one who calls them. The verse removes both the object of worship and the threat that protected it, and leaves the idolater with nothing to do and nothing to fear from.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:195

> **Have they feet with which they walk? Have they hands with which they grasp? Have they eyes with which they perceive? Have they ears with which they hear? Say, “Call upon your partners, then scheme against me, and grant me no respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Questions About the Senses**

The four questions run down the body: feet, hands, eyes, ears. Each is phrased identically and each expects the same answer — no. Ibn Kathīr and al-Māturīdī note the argument's shape: the capabilities of these idols fall short even of those enjoyed by the human beings who worship them, so how can one worship what is less capable than oneself?

The sequence is not random. Walking and grasping are powers of acting on the world; seeing and hearing are powers of receiving it. The idols have neither. They cannot do anything and they cannot register anything, which between them excludes every function a person might want from a deity — intervention and attention.

The preceding verse has already established the ground for the mockery. These false deities are *servants like* their worshippers (7:194), belonging to God and subject to His Sovereignty. The questions in verse 195 are the working-out of that claim: not a metaphysical demonstration but an inventory.

**The Challenge Issued**

> *Say, "Call upon your partners, then scheme against me, and grant me no respite."*

The Prophet is told to invite his opponents to their best effort — to call on the partners, plot against him, and give him no quarter. The formulation is identical or closely similar to challenges issued by earlier prophets: Noah at 10:71 and Hūd at 11:55 both tell their peoples to scheme against them and not hold back.

That repetition places this verse inside a prophetic pattern rather than treating it as a personal act of defiance. The same words are put in the mouth of God Himself at 77:39, addressing the deniers on the Day of Judgment: "So if you have a scheme, then scheme against Me!" The challenge scales from prophet to God without changing form.

The phrase *grant me no respite* (lā tunẓirūn) is the sharpest part. The challengers are asked not to delay, not to show restraint, not to give warning. Everything that would normally make an attack more likely to succeed is waived in advance, which is how the verse signals confidence that success is impossible regardless.

**Why the Scheme Is Known to Fail**

The invitation is understood to be safe because the schemes will ultimately fail: nothing in the heavens or on earth can thwart God (35:44). By contrast God's own scheme is firm, even while He grants them respite — see 7:182–83, where He extends respite to those who deny His signs while His plan proceeds.

The contrast with verse 15 in this same sūrah is exact and worth holding together. Iblīs asks for respite and receives it; the Prophet tells his opponents to give him none, because their refusal of respite would change nothing. Respite in the first case is a real grant with a real term; in the second it is irrelevant to the outcome. The same word does opposite work depending on who holds the power.

The verse is therefore not a taunt resting on personal courage. It rests on an assessment of what the opponents actually have: objects that cannot walk, grasp, see or hear, and whose worshipper is more capable than they are. The challenge is calibrated to that inventory.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:196

> **Truly my Protector is God, Who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Protector of the Prophet**

The verse states the statement of the Prophet ﷺ after the challenge of the scheme, and the statement is the statement that is the statement of the Protector: *Truly my Protector is God*. The invitation of the Prophet ﷺ to his detractors to scheme against him demonstrates his utter confidence in God's Succor and Protection, and the word for "Protector" of the verse is the Divine Name invoked several times in the Qur'an (4:45; 6:127; 42:9, 28; 59:23; 66:4), which in addition to "Protector" also means "Friend." The statement of the "Who sent down the Book" of the verse is the statement that is the teaching that the Protector of the Prophet ﷺ is the Protector who sent down the Book, and the Protector who sent down the Book is the Protector that is the teaching that the Book is the Book that is the Qur'an, and the Qur'an is the Qur'an that is the book of God that is the object of the protection of God, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 185 of the sūrah. The statement of the "and He protects the righteous" of the verse is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verses, in which God protects the reverent (45:19) and the believers (2:257; 3:68). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the protection of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the righteous and the believers and the reverent, and the people who are the objects of the protection of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the protection of God is the protection that is the protection of the righteous and the believers and the reverent, and the protection of the righteous and the believers and the reverent is the protection that is the teaching that the Protector of the Prophet ﷺ is the Protector of the people of the sūrah, and the Protector of the people of the sūrah is the Protector that is the teaching that the one who is the object of the protection of God is the one who is in the one who is the object of the mercy of God. The man who is the object of the statement of the verse is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the Protector of the man is the Protector that is God, and the Protector that is God is the Protector that is the teaching that the man who is the object of the protection of God is the man who is in the one who is the object of the next verse of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the Protector of the Prophet ﷺ and the statement of the protection of the righteous, and the statement of the Protector of the Prophet ﷺ and the statement of the protection of the righteous are the statement of the Protector of the Prophet ﷺ and the statement of the protection of the righteous that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the reiteration of the helplessness in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:197

> **And those whom you call upon apart from Him can neither help you, nor help themselves.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reiteration of the Helplessness**

The verse states the reiteration of the statement of the helplessness of the idols, and the reiteration is the reiteration that is on the form of the statement of verses 191–192 of the sūrah: *And those whom you call upon apart from Him can neither help you, nor help themselves*. The reiteration of the statement of the helplessness of the verse is the reiteration that is the emphasis of the teaching of the helplessness of the idols, and the emphasis of the teaching of the helplessness of the idols is the emphasis that is the teaching that the idol that is called upon apart from God is the idol that is the idol that cannot help the people who call upon it or help itself. The logic of the reiteration is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who call upon the idols apart from God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who call upon the objects that cannot help them or themselves, and the people who call upon the objects that cannot help them or themselves are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the reiteration of the teaching of the verse, and the people who are the objects of the reiteration of the teaching of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the object of the calling is the object that is the object that helps rather than the object that is the object that does not help, and the object of the calling that helps is the object that is God alone. The irony of the statement of the verse is the irony that is illustrated in the other verse (21:68), in which the idolaters among the people of Abraham seek to kill Abraham in order to "help their gods," and the irony of the verse is the irony that is the teaching that the people who kill the prophet in order to help their gods are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the gods of the people are the gods that are the gods that are the objects of the need of the help of the people who worship them, and the gods that are the objects of the need of the help of the people who worship them are the gods that are the idols rather than the gods. The man who calls upon the idol that cannot help him or itself is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping is the man who is in the one who is the object of the reiteration of the teaching of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the reiteration of the helplessness, and the statement of the reiteration of the helplessness is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the hearing not and the seeing not in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:198

> **If thou callest them unto guidance, they hear not. Thou seest them looking upon thee, but they see not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hearing Not and the Seeing Not**

The verse states the statement of the hearing not and the seeing not, and the statement is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verse (35:14): *If thou callest them unto guidance, they hear not. Thou seest them looking upon thee, but they see not*. According to most of the commentators, the "them" of the verse refers to the attempt to call the idols to the guidance, and the idols have been fashioned to appear as if they have the eyes, which are directed toward those in front of them, so that they seem to be looking upon those standing before them, but they see not. The other commentators mention the minority interpretation indicating that this verse refers to calling the idolaters, rather than the idols, unto the guidance. The logic of the ambiguity is the logic that is worth stating in full: the ambiguity that allows for the possibility that the verse is describing either the idols or the idolaters makes the strong rhetorical point, and the strong rhetorical point of the ambiguity is the strong rhetorical point that is the teaching that the reading of the verse in conjunction with verse 179 of the sūrah, which describes the idolaters as having the eyes, the ears, and the hearts that do not function so as to allow them to know and be rightly guided, and verse 195 of the sūrah, which describes the false idols they worship as without the functioning eyes and ears at all, suggests that in worshipping the blind and the deaf idols, the idolaters become similarly blind and deaf, or that the idolaters are already blind and deaf, otherwise they would not worship the idols. Although the idolaters, unlike the idols themselves, do have the physically functional eyes and ears, these faculties have become the atrophied and the spiritually dysfunctional, effectively making the idolaters as impervious to the guidance as the idols themselves. The man who worships the blind and the deaf idols is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the man who worships the blind and the deaf idols is the man who is in the one who is the blind and the deaf, and the man who is the blind and the deaf is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the hearing not and the seeing not, and the statement of the hearing not and the seeing not is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the command of the pardoning in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:199

> **Take to pardoning, and enjoin right, and turn away from the ignorant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of the Pardoning**

The verse states the three commands of God, and the three commands are the three commands that are the nobility of character: *Take to pardoning, and enjoin right, and turn away from the ignorant*. The command of the pardoning is on the form of the command of the other verses (2:109; 3:159; 5:13; 23:96–98; 24:22; 41:34–35; 45:14; 64:14), and the command means the forgiving and the excusing of the people's failings of character and deed and the being gentle rather than harsh. When this verse was revealed, the Prophet ﷺ reportedly asked the Archangel Gabriel what was meant, and after consulting with God, Gabriel stated that it meant that one should "maintain the ties of kinship with one who would cut himself off from you; give to the one who withholds from you; and pardon the one who wrongs you." The Prophet ﷺ also reportedly advised his followers, "Be easy; do not be difficult." The word for "pardoning" of the verse can also mean "a surplus," and the minority interpretation understands this verse as commanding the Prophet ﷺ to collect the surplus wealth of his followers for the purposes of charity prior to the divine commandment to establish the obligatory alms.

**The Command of the Enjoining of the Right**

The command of the enjoining of the right of the verse is the command that is found in the other verses, although in these cases the word for "right" translates the word that means the good, while here it translates the related term that means what is considered right according to custom or tradition, and both terms connote the proper, the normative, and the honorable behavior (3:104, 110, 114; 7:157; 9:71, 112; 22:41; 31:17). The logic of the command of the enjoining of the right is the logic that is on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 157 of the sūrah, in which the Prophet ﷺ is described as the one who enjoins upon the people what is right and forbids them what is wrong.

**The Command of the Turning Away from the Ignorant**

The command of the turning away from the ignorant of the verse is the command that means to be patient with the foolish or the bad behavior, to avert one's eyes from the behavior that is offensive, and to leave the people alone once one has apprised them of the truth, rather than arguing with them or responding to them in kind, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (4:63; 6:68, 106, 112, 137; 9:95; 15:94; 32:30; 43:83; 51:54; 70:42). The verse thus sums up the attitudes that represent the nobility of character. Although some have suggested that the verses that counsel the forbearance toward the idolaters or the disbelievers were abrogated by the later verses that commanded the Prophet ﷺ to fight his religious opponents, the other commentators argue that this is not the case, since the injunction toward the gentleness and the leniency with one's religious opponents does not stand in contradiction to the command to fight them when necessary or to claim one's rights when justified, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (42:40), in which it is stated that the recompense of an evil is an evil like unto it, yet whosoever pardons and sets the matters aright, his reward is with God, and truly God loves not the wrongdoers. The logic of the three commands of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the three commands are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the pardoning is the pardoning that is the forgiveness of the failings of the people, and the enjoining of the right is the enjoining that is the enjoining of the proper and the normative and the honorable behavior, and the turning away from the ignorant is the turning away that is the patience with the foolish or the bad behavior and the leaving of the people alone once one has apprised them of the truth, and the three attitudes of the verse are the three attitudes that are the nobility of character. The man who is the object of the three commands of the verse is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the nobility of character is the nobility that is the pardoning and the enjoining of the right and the turning away from the ignorant, and the man who is the object of the teaching is the man who is in the one who is the example of the nobility of character. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the three commands of the nobility of character, and the statement of the three commands of the nobility of character is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the seeking of the refuge in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:200

> **And should a temptation from Satan provoke thee, seek refuge in God. Truly He is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to the Prophet That Applies to Everyone**

> *And should a temptation from Satan provoke thee, seek refuge in God. Truly He is Hearing, Knowing.*

An identical verse appears at 41:36. Al-Rāzī notes that although this one is addressed to the Prophet, the injunction it contains applies to all believers — the address fixes the occasion, not the scope.

In immediate context, al-Ṭabarī reads it specifically: if one is provoked by a Satanic temptation to respond with anger to the ignorant ones mentioned in verse 199, rather than turning away from them in patience, one should seek refuge in God. Verse 199 had enjoined holding to pardon, commanding what is customary, and turning away from the ignorant. Verse 200 supplies the emergency procedure for when that instruction starts to fail.

The application is nevertheless general, since Muslims are always encouraged to seek God's help against Satanic provocation in light of Satan's declared threat to mislead human beings — at 7:16–17, 15:39 and 17:62.

**Why Anger Is Named as the Vulnerability**

The specific provocation in context is anger at people behaving ignorantly. That is a precise diagnosis rather than a generic warning. Anger at provocation feels like a response to the other person, and the verse treats it as a response to something working through the other person.

The connection to verse 199 makes the mechanics visible. Turn away from the ignorant is achievable when one is not provoked; it becomes very difficult at the moment of provocation, and provocation is exactly what the verse anticipates. The command to seek refuge is placed where patience is hardest rather than where it is easy.

Naming a single concrete failure point is more useful than a general exhortation to resist evil. The reader is given a situation to recognise — the surge of anger at someone behaving badly — and an action to take inside it.

**The Istiʿādhah in Practice**

The formula "I seek refuge in God from the outcast Satan" (*aʿūdhu bi'Llāhi min al-shayṭān al-rajīm*) is frequently uttered in Muslim daily life, not only when one fears evil forces or temptations but at the beginning of various ritual and religious acts. Known as the *istiʿādhah*, it precedes the *basmalah* when making ablutions, praying, or reciting Qur'anic verses.

The Qur'an enjoins this sequence explicitly for recitation at 16:98: "And when thou recitest the Quran, seek refuge in God from the outcast Satan." The practice therefore is not a later devotional elaboration but a Qur'anic instruction that has been carried into daily use.

Its placement at the start of acts of worship is worth reflecting on. Before reciting, one asks for protection — which concedes that the recitation itself can be corrupted by inattention, vanity, or the impulse to perform rather than receive. The istiʿādhah is an acknowledgement of vulnerability at the moment one is about to do something that requires the absence of it.

**Hearing, Knowing**

The verse closes with two Divine Names: *Truly He is Hearing, Knowing* — Samīʿ, ʿAlīm. Both bear on why seeking refuge is the prescribed response rather than resisting unaided.

Hearing addresses the act itself: the petition is spoken, and God hears it. Knowing addresses what the petitioner cannot see — the provenance of the provocation, its aim, and the state of the heart in which it lands. A person experiencing a surge of anger is characteristically a poor judge of what produced it, which is why the verse pairs the command with the Name that covers the ground he cannot survey.

The two Names together justify the instruction's form. It is not "master your anger" but "seek refuge in God," because the situation is one in which self-mastery is precisely what is under attack. The verse routes the response outward to a Hearing and Knowing agent rather than asking the provoked person to be calmer by an act of will.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:201

> **Truly those who are reverent, when they are touched by a visitation from Satan, they remember; then behold, they see.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Visitation of Satan**

The verse states the state of the reverent when they are touched by a visitation from Satan, and the state is the state that is the two acts: the remembering, and the seeing: *Truly those who are reverent, when they are touched by a visitation from Satan, they remember; then behold, they see*. The statement of the "to be touched by a visitation from Satan" of the verse means to be tempted by Satan, perhaps in a dream or apparition, which is the lexical meaning of the word for "visitation." Some of the commentators consider a visitation from Satan to refer here to the impulse toward the anger, or to any Satanic insinuation, the "whispering," that tempts one toward the moral or the intellectual lapse. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are the objects of the visitation of Satan are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are tempted by the Satanic whispering, and the people who are tempted by the Satanic whispering are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the state of the reverent in the state of the visitation, and the state of the reverent in the state of the visitation is the state that is the state of the remembering and the seeing.

**The Remembering and the Seeing**

The verse states the two acts of the reverent, and the two acts are the two acts that are the remembering, and the seeing. The reverent, when faced with such a Satanic visitation or temptation, remember, that is, they remember God, and in particular they remember the commands of God and the threat of His punishment, and so return to the obedience. The word for "remember" of the verse can also mean to "reflect" or to "take heed." After the remembering, the reverent again see, meaning that they are restored to the sound judgment and the guidance, and return to what is right and what God has commanded. The commentary of al-Zamakhsharī states that the "remembering" God in this way whenever one is tempted is the regular habit of the reverent. The logic of the two acts of the reverent is the logic that is worth stating in full: the reverent who remember in the state of the visitation are the reverent who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are restored to the sound judgment and the guidance, and the reverent who are restored to the sound judgment and the guidance are the reverent who are in the state of the one who are the ones who see, and the reverent who see are the reverent who are in the state of the one who are the ones who return to what is right and what God has commanded. The man who is the object of the visitation of Satan and who remembers is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the remembering of God in the state of the temptation is the remembering that is the antidote of the Satanic temptation, and the reminding of God in the state of the temptation is the reminding that is the regular habit of the reverent, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the previous verse, in which the verse states the command of the seeking of the refuge in God. The man who is the object of the teaching of the verse is the man who is in the one who is the example of the reverent in the state of the visitation of Satan. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the state of the reverent in the state of the visitation of Satan, and the statement of the state of the reverent in the state of the visitation of Satan is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the contrast of the brethren in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:202

> **But as for their brethren, they draw them ever further into error, and then they cease not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Contrast of the Brethren**

The verse states the state of the "brethren" in contrast to the state of the reverent of the previous verse, and the state is the state that is the drawing into the error: *But as for their brethren, they draw them ever further into error, and then they cease not*. The "their brethren" of the verse is the "brethren" of Satan, or the satans, since Satan can be the collective reference to the satans as a group, and may be the reference to the ignorant of verse 199 of the sūrah, or to the idolaters in general (2:14). The logic of the contrast is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the drawing into the error are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who, in contrast to the reverent of the previous verse, allow themselves to be drawn ever further into the error, and the people who allow themselves to be drawn ever further into the error are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are literally helped along in their error by the Satanic temptation, until they err continuously, having no fear of God to give them pause. The other reading of the verse is the reading that the satans do not cease in their attempts to lead such people further into the error, and the two readings of the verse are the two readings that are the teaching that the state of the people is the state that is the drawing into the error by the satans, or the state that is the drawing into the error by the people themselves. The man who is the object of the drawing into the error is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the man who is drawn into the error is the man who is in the one who is the object of the ceasing not in the error, and the man who is the object of the state of the ceasing not in the error is the man who is in the one who is the object of the contrast of the verse with the state of the reverent of the previous verse, and the contrast of the verse with the state of the reverent of the previous verse is the contrast that is the teaching that the state of the man is the state that is the state of the reverent in the state of the visitation or the state that is the state of the drawing into the error in the state of the visitation, and the two states of the man in the state of the visitation are the two states that are the teaching that the state of the man is the state that is the response of the man to the visitation of Satan. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the contrast of the brethren, and the statement of the contrast of the brethren is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the request of the sign in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:203

> **And when thou bringest them not a sign, they say, “Why do you not choose it?” Say, “I only follow that which is revealed unto me from my Lord. These are insights from your Lord, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request of the Sign**

The verse states the act of the disbelievers when the Prophet ﷺ does not bring them a sign, and the act is the act that is the question: *And when thou bringest them not a sign, they say, "Why do you not choose it?"* The request of the sign of the disbelievers from the prophets who had been sent to them is the theme that is repeated throughout the Qur'an, and the question of the verse is the question that is the assumption that the signs or the miracles brought by the prophets are the matters of their own choosing, and that the Prophet ﷺ can simply select at will a sign to present to them. The other reading of the question is the reading that the idolaters are asking, "Why have you not received a sign from your Lord?" The response that the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to give is the response that is the statement of the following of the revelation: *I only follow that which is revealed unto me from my Lord*. The response of the verse is similar to the other statements the Prophet ﷺ makes indicating that whatever power or knowledge he has is limited to what God has given him, for example, that he is simply the warner and the bearer of glad tidings, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 188 of the sūrah. The logic of the response is the logic that is worth stating in full: the prophet who responds that he only follows that which is revealed unto him from his Lord is the prophet who is in the one who demonstrates the limitation of the power and the knowledge of the prophet to what God has given him, and the prophet who demonstrates the limitation of the power and the knowledge of the prophet to what God has given him is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the sign of the prophet is the sign that is the sign that is the object of the revelation of God rather than the sign that is the sign that is the object of the choice of the prophet, and the sign that is the object of the revelation of God is the sign that is the teaching that the request of the sign the choice of the prophet is the request that is the request that is refuted by the response of the prophet.

**The Insights of the Lord**

The verse closes with the statement of the insights of the Lord, and the statement is the statement that is the three descriptions: *These are insights from your Lord, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe*. The "insights from your Lord" of the verse refers to the Quranic verses, which are revealed to the Prophet ﷺ, and they are further described as the guidance and the mercy. The same three descriptions are given of the Qur'an in the other verse (45:20) and of the Torah in the other verse (28:43), and elsewhere, both the Qur'an and the Torah are described as the guidance and the mercy (6:154; 7:52, 154; 10:57; 16:64, 89; 27:77; 31:3). According to the commentary of Ibn Kathīr, the response of the Prophet ﷺ to the request of the sign of the idolaters concludes with the reference to the Qur'an in order to indicate that the Quranic verses themselves are the greatest miracle or "sign" of the Prophet ﷺ. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are the objects of the request of the sign are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the greatest sign of the prophet is the book of God that is revealed to the prophet, and the book of God that is revealed to the prophet is the book that is the book that is the insights of God and the guidance and the mercy for the people who believe, and the book that is the insights of God and the guidance and the mercy for the people who believe is the book that is the teaching that the people who believe are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the guidance and the mercy of the book of God. The man who is the object of the request of the sign is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the sign of the prophet is the sign that is the book of God, and the man who is the object of the teaching is the man who is in the one who is the object of the next section of the sūrah, in which the verse states the command of the listening to the recitation of the Qur'an. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the request of the sign and the statement of the insights of the Lord, and the statement of the request of the sign and the statement of the insights of the Lord are the statement of the request of the sign and the statement of the insights of the Lord that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the command of the listening to the recitation of the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:204

> **And when the Quran is recited, hearken unto it, and listen, that haply you may receive mercy.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Verbs Where One Would Do**

> *And when the Qur'an is recited, hearken unto it, and listen, that haply you may receive mercy.*

The verse pairs two near-synonyms, *hearken* (istamiʿū) and *listen* (anṣitū). Al-Ālusī and al-Ṭabarī hold that the pairing is for emphasis, and the emphasis is on the *care* with which the recited Qur'an is to be received — full attention given both to understanding its meaning and to reflecting on its teachings, with al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī adding that reflection is incomplete without acting on what the text prescribes.

The two verbs are not simply redundant, and the difference between them is where the instruction lives. Anṣitū connotes listening *silently*; commentators note it was particularly important while the Prophet himself was reciting. Istamiʿū is the wider act of attending. So the verse asks for attention and for quiet, and the quiet is not incidental — it is the condition under which attention becomes possible.

The threefold shape al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī give the command is worth holding onto: hear, understand, act. Each stage depends on the one before it. A person who has not genuinely heard cannot understand; one who has not understood cannot act on it; and acting is what makes the hearing count. The verse compresses all three into a single instruction about listening.

**Silence in the Congregation**

The verse has a concrete legal application that al-Ālusī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī and al-Zamakhsharī all record: it governs the etiquette of communal prayer. Those praying behind the imām are to be silent and attend to the Qur'anic passages he recites — neither conversing with fellow worshippers nor reciting along with him.

This is a good example of a Qur'anic command settling a practical dispute by addressing the underlying disposition rather than the surface behaviour. The question of whether the congregation recites aloud behind the imām was contested among the early jurists, and the verse is one of the principal texts cited in that discussion. What it establishes beyond the technical ruling is that the congregation's role during recitation is receptive, not performative.

Some commentators extend the command past the prayer entirely: al-Ālusī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī hold that attentive listening applies in any context where one hears the Qur'an recited. On that reading the prayer ruling is the clearest instance of a general discipline rather than its limit.

**Listening as Servitude**

Two further readings push inward. Some Sufi commentators take silent, purely receptive listening as a means of developing pure servitude toward God — a practice in which the listener stops supplying anything and simply receives. Al-Ālusī frames it as coming into the Presence of God, who is the ultimate Speaker behind the recited words.

That framing changes what listening is for. If God is the real Speaker, then the recitation is not a performance being evaluated but an address being received, and the appropriate posture is the one a person takes when someone of consequence speaks directly to him. The silence the verse requires is the silence of that posture.

**Why Mercy Follows Listening**

The closing clause — *that haply you may receive mercy* — connects back to verse 203, where the Qur'an is itself described as a mercy. The logic is straightforward: mercy is present in the recited words, so attending to them is how one receives it. Ibn ʿAjībah states it compactly: "mercy is the closest thing to the one listening to the Qur'an."

The particle *laʿalla* ("haply", "that perhaps") is characteristic of Qur'anic usage for Divine outcomes. It marks the result as genuinely attainable while making clear that it is not mechanically produced. Listening does not compel mercy; it places a person where mercy is being given. The distinction matters, because it rules out treating the practice as a transaction with a guaranteed return.

Practically, this describes something most readers recognise. A text can be read past repeatedly without landing, and then at some point attended to properly and change a person. The difference between those two events is not in the text. The verse is legislating the difference.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:205

> **And remember thy Lord within thy soul, humbly and in awe, being not loud of voice, in the morning and the evening, and be not among those who are heedless.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of the Remembrance**

The verse states the command of God with regard to the remembrance of God, and the command is the command that is the remembrance within the soul: *And remember thy Lord within thy soul*. The command to remember thy Lord of the verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ, but applies to all the people. The command likely refers to both the invocation of God with the tongue and the remembrance of God in the heart, the latter indicated by the call to remember God within thy soul, and this "remembrance" may also take the form of the reciting of the Qur'an or the uttering of the words of praise and glorification for God as well as the testament to His Oneness. Read in connection with the previous verse, the remembrance of thy Lord may be the injunction to remember God when one hears the Qur'an being recited. The "to remember God within thy soul" of the verse may also mean to remember Him "within thyself," and the commentary of al-Ṭabarī connects this verse with the famous sacred hadith, "When My servant remembers Me in himself, I remember him in Myself; when he remembers Me in solitude, I remember him in solitude; when he remembers Me in an assembly, I remember him in an assembly that is nobler." The present verse is one of the most important in suggesting the spiritual importance of the remembrance or the invocation, encouraged, particularly by the Sufis, as the means of attaining an "unveiling" by which one arrives at the more profound understanding of God, and realizes that God is ever the near and the witness to one's thoughts and deeds, and indeed as the means of the experiencing of His Presence and one's immersion "in the ocean of Divinity." According to the hadith, "All that is on the earth is accursed save the remembrance of God."

**The Humble and the Secret Remembrance**

The command to remember God humbly and in awe, being not loud of voice, of the verse is similar to the command to call upon Him humbly and in secret, and can be understood as the directions for the performance of the remembrance in the Sufism. The lowering of the voice is also enjoined during the supplicatory prayer, when speaking in the presence of the Prophet ﷺ, and as part of one's general comportment, for the vilest of the voices are the voices of the asses. To pray quietly reflects the sincerity in one's prayer, indicating that one is not praying to be seen of men, and is the best suited to facilitating the reflection and the recollection. The Prophet ﷺ was once asked by some of the Companions whether God was the near, so that they should whisper to Him, or the far, so that they should shout, after which the other verse (2:186) was then revealed, in which God states, "When My servants ask thee about Me, truly I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls Me." The commentary of al-Rāzī explains that the invocation or the remembrance enjoined here should be done not silently, but in the voice low enough that it can be heard only by oneself, since there is the profound connection between the body, the soul, and the spirit, each of which is the door to the others, a connection that is activated by the process of the remembering or the invoking of God, and the hearing of the invocation uttered by the bodily tongue moves the imagination and the soul of the one invoking, which in turn strengthens the remembrance in the spiritual heart, and the act of the remembrance thus takes place simultaneously on all the three levels, each reflecting upon and strengthening the others. Because of the injunction to remember God quietly, some might consider this verse as the prohibition of the loud, the communal remembrance practiced by the Sufi groups, however, the commentary of Ibn ʿAjībah rejects this interpretation, noting that this verse was revealed in Makkah, when the believers had to be the circumspect in their worship, and that when the community migrated to Madinah, the Prophet ﷺ and the Companions would conduct the public remembrance sessions in the mosque.

**The Morning and the Evening**

The "morning and the evening" of the verse are mentioned here and elsewhere as the times particularly suited to the remembering, the supplicating, or the praising of God (3:41; 6:52; 18:28; 19:11; 24:36; 33:41–42; 48:9; 76:25), and these two times of day may also be mentioned as the way of the saying that one should remember and praise God at all the times. These two times, marking the beginning and the end of the day, are mentioned specifically because of the spiritual symbolism they entail, each being the time of the transition between the light and the darkness, the sleeping and the waking, and thus the suggestive of the death and the resurrection.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:206

> **Surely those who are with thy Lord are not too arrogant to worship Him. And they glorify Him, and prostrate unto Him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those Who Are with Thy Lord**

> *Surely those who are with thy Lord are not too arrogant to worship Him. And they glorify Him, and prostrate unto Him.*

*Those who are with thy Lord* are understood by al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī to be the angels. Their being "with" God is not spatial — it is nearness to His Mercy and Bounty. The sūrah closes, as it opened, by locating the whole of creation in relation to God, and the highest creatures in that order are described first as worshippers.

The verse is the last in the sūrah, and its placement is deliberate. Al-Aʿrāf has spent two hundred verses on human refusal: the chiefs who rejected their messengers, Pharaoh's establishment, the people who demanded an idol before their feet were dry. The final verse answers all of it with the angels, who are under no such temptation and worship anyway.

**The Argument from the Greater to the Lesser**

Al-Rāzī draws out the implication explicitly: if the angels, with their spiritual purity and nearness to God, are not too arrogant to worship Him, how can human beings — marked by spiritual imperfection and distance from God — be reluctant to worship Him?

This is an argument from the greater to the lesser, and its force depends on getting the direction right. Worship is not a burden that lesser creatures can be excused from because it exceeds their capacity. It is a good that the highest creatures pursue without needing to. The angels have nothing to gain from it and lose nothing by it, which is precisely why their worship establishes that worship is not about extraction.

The vice named here is *kibr* — arrogance, the same word used for Iblīs's refusal earlier in this sūrah. The Qur'an's diagnosis of unbelief is consistently that it is not an intellectual failure but a posture. Iblīs did not lack evidence; he declined to submit. The angels are set at the opposite pole: capable of the same refusal, and not making it.

Al-Rāzī points to 4:172 as the parallel text — "the Messiah will never disdain to be a servant of God, nor the angels who are near Him" — where disdain (an yastankif) plays exactly the role arrogance plays here. Both verses treat worship as something the exalted are not above, and both identify the refusal as a matter of self-regard.

**Glorification and Prostration**

The verse names two acts. They *glorify* Him — yusabbiḥūn, from s-b-ḥ, declaring God free of every deficiency. Constant angelic praise is a recurring Qur'anic theme, and al-Rāzī cites 13:13, "the angels hymn the praise of their Lord," 39:75, "thou seest the angels hymning the praise of their Lord," and 42:5, "the angels hymn the praise of their Lord and ask forgiveness for those on earth."

Then they *prostrate*. Al-Rāzī notes 16:49: "And unto God prostrateth whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on earth, of beast and angel, and they are not arrogant" — which repeats this verse's denial of arrogance and extends the prostration to all creation.

The pairing of glorification with prostration joins the inward declaration to the outward posture. Tasbīḥ is said; sujūd is done with the whole body. The angels are described as doing both, which is the pattern the sūrah asks of its reader.

**A Verse of Prostration**

Because this verse mentions prostrating, it is one of the fifteen verses known as the *sajdah* verses. They are marked in official Arabic texts of the Qur'an, indicating that Muslims should physically prostrate upon reciting them or hearing them recited — though this is not required by Islamic Law. See the note at 19:58.

The arrangement is striking and it is not accidental. The final word of the sūrah describes prostration, and the received practice of the community is to enact it at that point. The text does not merely report that the angels prostrate; it becomes the occasion for the reader to do the same. Reading the last verse aloud in the prescribed way ends the sūrah with the reader on the ground in the posture the verse describes.

That is the sūrah's whole argument in a single gesture. Two hundred verses of human refusal close with an invitation to join the angels rather than Iblīs, and the physical act is the answer the reader gives. The distinction between the two is not settled by what one believes about God but by whether one bows.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Aʿrāf]**
